Controlling Moments with Kernel Stein Discrepancies
Heishiro Kanagawa, Alessandro Barp, Arthur Gretton, Lester Mackey

Abstract
Kernel Stein discrepancies (KSDs) measure the quality of a distributional approximation and can be computed even when the target density has an intractable normalizing constant. Notable applications include the diagnosis of approximate MCMC samplers and goodness-of-fit tests for unnormalized statistical models. The present work analyzes the convergence control properties of KSDs. We first show that standard KSDs used for weak convergence control fail to control moment convergence. To address this limitation, we next provide sufficient conditions under which alternative diffusion KSDs control both moment and weak convergence. As an immediate consequence we develop, for each q > 0 q>0 , the first KSDs known to exactly characterize q q -Wasserstein convergence.



1
Introduction

Consider the problem of computing the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]=\int f(x)\differential P(x)

of a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to a probability
distribution
P.

We will specifically focus on the scenario where
P
is defined by a density function with an unknown normalization constant.
In such cases, exact computation of the expectation is typically intractable, necessitating the use of approximation methods.
Numerical integration methods produce estimates of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}f(x_{i})
by combining evaluations of the integrand.
The associated weights
w_{i}
and points
x_{i}
can be considered as an approximating probability distribution
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1
and
w_{i}\geq 0
.
Various techniques are available, and foremost among them is Markov chain Monte Carlo
(
Liu 2004
, MCMC,)
ensuring accurate estimates as
n
tends to infinity.

The focus of this paper is on assessing the quality of such sample approximations.
Our pursuit is motivated by the following two considerations.
First, in practice, it is challenging to vouch for approximation quality at
finite
sample sizes, even for established methods like MCMC.
Second,
practitioners are
increasingly turning to asymptotically inexact methods that offer greater computational convenience at the expense of persistent inferential bias.
One illustrative example can be found in modern Bayesian statistics, where
exact MCMC becomes computationally unattractive due to a large number of datapoint terms or factors in the likelihood. In this setting, cheaper but asymptotically biased alternatives, such as approximate MCMC
(
Welling and Teh 2011
;
Ma et al. 2015
;
Dalalyan 2017
;
Durmus and Moulines 2017
)
or variational Bayesian methods
(
Blei et al. 2017
)
, are often employed.
Regardless of the type of method used, quantifying the quality
of approximation is desirable since we can then diagnose and compare candidate methods to obtain more accurate estimates.
This objective demands a quality measure that is computable for a broad range of approximation methods.

This problem may be approached using
Stein discrepancies
, a
class of measures of discrepancy between probability distributions

(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)
. The key idea behind a Stein discrepancy is that
we can construct functions that are integrated to zero under the target distribution;
such functions may be used to measure the discrepancy of another distribution,
since a non-zero expectation under the distribution indicates its
deviation from the target. Formally, a Stein discrepancy of a distribution

Q,
with respect to target
P,
is defined as the worst-case quantity

\sup_{g\in{\cal{\cal G}}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{Q}[{\cal T}_{P}g]\right|,

where
{\cal T}_{P}
and
{\cal G}
are a
Stein operator

and a
Stein set
(a function class in the domain of
{\cal T}_{P}
),
inducing functions
{\cal T}_{P}g
whose expectations vanish under

P.
Different choices of the operator and the function class yield
distinct Stein discrepancies. A prototypical example is the Langevin
Stein operator
(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Oates et al. 2017
)
, which may be defined
without the normalization constant; and two major classes based on
this operator are the graph Stein discrepancies
(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
)

and the kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD)
(
Chwialkowski et al. 2016
;
Liu et al. 2016
;
Oates et al. 2017
;
Gorham and Mackey 2017
)
.
Notably, it is possible to compute these discrepancies: the KSD circumvents
the supremum in the definition and admits a closed-form expression
involving kernel evaluations on samples, whereas the graph Stein discrepancy
involves solving a linear program.
This computational advantage has sparked numerous statistical applications involving unnormalzed densities
(see the survey by
Anastasiou et al. 2023
, for an overview)
.

While successfully avoiding the intractable expectation, Stein discrepancies
may not be immediately interpreted in terms of the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]

of interest. Prior work therefore investigated how Stein discrepancies
characterize closeness in expectation under suitable regularity conditions
on the integrand.
With the exception of
Barp et al. 2024
, the foregoing studies used Stein’s method
(
Stein 1972
)

to relate Stein discrepancies to integral probability metrics (IPMs)

(
Müller 1997
)
, which measure the worst-case difference
in expectations with respect to given classes of functions.
Gorham and Mackey 2015

showed that the Langevin graph Stein discrepancy controls the 1-Wasserstein
distance (the IPM defined by
1
-Lipschitz functions) for distantly
dissipative target distributions;
Gorham et al. 2019
later
generalized this result to heavy-tailed targets with diffusion graph
Stein discrepancies.
Gorham and Mackey 2017
proved that the Langevin
KSD with the inverse multi-quadratic kernel (IMQ) controls the bounded-Lipschitz
metric;
Chen et al. 2018
offer other kernel choices.
Feature Stein discrepancies by
Huggins and Mackey 2018
can control the bounded-Lipschitz
metric, with estimators computable in linear time with respect to the sample size.
Using a distinct approach from the aforementioned works,
Barp et al. 2024

showed that the separating property of the KSD is equivalent to having
(tight) weak convergence control (i.e., convergence in expectations
of bounded continuous functions).

Statistical analysis often requires handling unbounded functions; e.g., basic
statistical measures such as mean and variance are the expectation
of linear and quadratic functions.
That said, it remains an open question
to which class of unbounded integrands, Stein discrepancies are guaranteed to indicate the quality of expectation approximations.
This question has been in part tackled by works
on graph Stein discrepancies
(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
;
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.
These analyses, however, are limited to linearly growing functions
– it is unclear how these results extend to functions of faster
growth. Moreover, despite its computational appeal over the graph Stein discrepancies,
the KSD has only been shown to control weak convergence.
These observations highlight a gap in our understanding of the broader applicability of Stein discrepancies.

This article aims to extend the reach of the KSD to functions of arbitrary
polynomial growth. In particular, we investigate conditions under
which the KSD controls the convergence of expectations of polynomially
growing continuous functions (
q
-Wasserstein convergence
, defined formally in Section
3.1
).
Our specific contributions are threefold.
First, our analysis considers the diffusion kernel Stein discrepancy
(
Barp et al. 2019
)
,
a generalization of the Langevin KSD
(
Chwialkowski et al. 2016
;
Liu et al. 2016
;
Oates et al. 2017
;
Gorham and Mackey 2017
)
. This extension allows us to consider a broader target class, including heavy-tailed distributions, for which the Langevin KSD has proved inadequate to control weak convergence
(
Gorham and Mackey 2017
;
Barp et al. 2024
)
.
Second, we show that standard KSDs used for controlling weak convergence fail to control moment convergence. As our third contribution, we address this limitation by identifying, for the first time, specific practical reproducing kernels that provide polynomial convergence control.
Consequently, we establish the first Stein discrepancies known to exactly control
q
-Wasserstein convergence for each
q>0
.

The rest of the article is organized as follows.
Section
2
introduces the KSD and its associated Stein operator.

Section

3
presents the motivating negative result of standard KSDs, followed by two main results addressing this shortcoming.
In
Section

3.1
,
our first result, building on the work of
Barp et al. 2024
, establishes sufficient conditions on the kernel to facillitate the control of
q
-Wasserstein convergence by the KSD.
In Section
3.2
,
building on the finite Stein factor results of
Erdogdu et al. 2018
, our
second result provides an explicit KSD upper bound on the IPM defined
by a class of pseudo-Lipschitz functions, a polynomial generalization
of Lipschitz functions. This bound enables us to describe a rate of Wasserstein convergence in terms of the KSD
and to obtain an explicit bound on the
q
-Wasserstein distance.
Our experiments in
Section

4
confirm the claimed convergence
control property. We conclude in Section
5
.

Notation and definitions

We define the inner product
\langle A,B\rangle
between multi-dimensional arrays
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\dots\times d_{n}}

by the sum of the elementwise product
\sum A_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}B_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
, where
A_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
and
B_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
are the
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
-entry of the respective arrays.
For
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\dots\times d_{n}}
, we define its Frobenius norm by
\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=\sqrt{\langle A,A\rangle}

and its operator norm by

\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\coloneqq\sup_{\lVert u^{(1)}\rVert_{2}=\cdots=\lVert u^{(n)}\rVert_{2}=1}\lvert\langle A,u^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes u^{(n)}\rangle\rvert,

where
u^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes u^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{n}}

denotes the outer product of
(u^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}})_{i=1}^{n}
; i.e.,

(u^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes u^{(n)})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}=u^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\cdots u^{(n)}_{i_{n}}.

The Euclidean norm of
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\lVert a\rVert_{2}(=\lVert a\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=\lVert a\rVert_{\mathrm{F}})
.
With
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{d})^{\top}
,
we define
\nabla^{i}
to act on
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
such that
\nabla^{i}g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{(\times_{k=1}^{i}d)\times d^{\prime}}
and
(\nabla^{i}g(x))_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i},j}=\partial_{j_{1}}\cdots\partial_{j_{i}}g_{j}(x)
,
where
\partial_{i}
stands for the partial derivative with respect to the
i
th coordinate.
The divergence of a vector-valued function
{v}(x)=({v}_{1}(x),\dots,{v}_{d}(x))
is defined as
\langle\nabla,{v}(x)\rangle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}{v}_{i}(x)
.
For
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, we define a vector-matrix product
\langle v,A\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
by
(\langle v,A\rangle)_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{d_{1}}v_{j}A_{ji}
.
Following this notation, for a matrix-valued function
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we use
\langle\nabla,A(x)\rangle
to denote its column-wise divergence, i.e.,
\langle\nabla,A(x)\rangle_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{j}A_{ji}(x)
.
For a two-argument function
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\partial_{\ell,i}K(a,b)

to be the elementwise partial derivative of
K
at
(a,b)
taken with respect to the
\ell
th argument and its
i
th coordinate.

We denote by
{\cal P}
the set of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Both
\int f\differential\mu
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]
stand for the expectation of a function
f
with
respect to
\mu\in{\cal P}.

For
k\geq 0
, the symbol
\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
stands for the set of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued

k
-times continuously differentiable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d},

and we simply write
{\cal C}^{k}
for
{\cal\mathcal{C}}^{k}(\mathbb{R}).

We also define the set
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of
functions whose
j
th partial derivatives vanish at infinity for any
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
,
which is equipped with the norm

f\mapsto\max_{\lvert\bm{i}\rvert\leq k}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\partial^{\bm{i}}f(x)\rVert_{2}
,
where
\partial^{\bm{i}}=\partial_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{d}^{i_{d}}
with
\bm{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
and
\lvert\bm{i}\rvert=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{d}
.
We define
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mu)=\{f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}:\int\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential\mu<\infty\};

when
n=1,
we similarly use the shorthand
L^{q}(\mu).

The maximum (resp. minimum) of two real numbers
a,b
is denoted
by
a\lor b
(resp.
a\land b
); in particular, we use
(x)_{+}

to denote
(0\lor x)
.
Similarly, for a real-valued function
f
, we define
f^{+}(x)=(f(x)\lor 0)
and
f^{-}(x)=-(f(x)\land 0)
.
For a set
S
and
A\subset S
, we define the associated indicator function
1_{A}:S\to\mathbb{R}
to yield
1
on
A
and
0
otherwise. If
A\subset S
is given in the form
\{s:S:f(s)\in B\}
, we also use the shorthand
1\{f(s)\in B\}
.
The (order-
0
)
generalized Fourier transform

(
Wendland 2004
, Definition 8.9)
of (polynomially-increasing) function
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as a measurable function
\hat{\Phi}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following: (a)
\hat{\Phi}
is square integrable on every compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and (b)
\int\Phi(x)\hat{\gamma}(x)\differential x=\int\hat{\Phi}(x)\gamma(x)\differential x
, where
\gamma
is an arbitrary Schwartz function and
\hat{\gamma}
denotes its Fourier transform.

2
Diffusion kernel Stein discrepancies

In this section, we recall the definition of the kernel Stein discrepancy
(KSD), a discrepancy measure derived by combining a diffusion Stein
operator and a reproducing kernel Hilbert space.
In the process, we introduce our notation and assumptions
made throughout the paper.

The
diffusion Stein operator

(
Gorham et al. 2019
;
Barp et al. 2019
)

is a class of Stein operators that characterizes probability distributions
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
d\geq 1.
Suppose
P
is a probability
distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d},
defined by a density function
p

with respect to the Lebesgue measure; we assume that
p
is differentiable
and positive everywhere. Following
Barp et al. 2019
,
we define a diffusion Stein operator
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}
as an operator
that takes as input a vector-valued differentiable function
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

and outputs a real-valued function as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(x)}\langle\nabla,p(x)m(x)v(x)\rangle,

(1)

where
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a differentiable
matrix-valued function called a
diffusion matrix
.
A specific choice
of the diffusion matrix defines a particular Stein operator. The
Langevin
Stein operator

(
Gorham and Mackey 2015
)
is a special case of this class
and is given by taking the identity matrix
m\equiv\mathrm{Id}.
With
an appropriate choice, we may relate the operator to the generator
of an Itô diffusion process, which will be critical in our analysis
in Section
3.2
.
By the divergence theorem
(
Pigola and Setti 2014
, Theorem 2.36)
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v\bigr]=0
provided
mv\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},P)
,
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v\in L^{1}(P)
, and
pmv\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Thus, the operator indeed generates zero-mean functions. In the rest
of the paper, we assume
p\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
m\in{\cal C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}).

A Stein discrepancy is obtained once
we specify a function class to be paired with a diffusion Stein operator.
The KSD is a Stein discrepancy formed with functions from a
reproducing kernel Hilbert space
(RKHS)
(
Aronszajn 1950
;
Schwartz 1964
)
.
Specifically, we make use of an RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{K}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions.
For a set
\mathcal{X}
, a matrix-valued function
K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is called a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-reproducing kernel if it satisfies
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\langle a_{i},K(x_{i},x_{j})a_{j}\rangle\geq 0
for all
m\geq 1
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathcal{X}
, and
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A kernel
K
uniquely determines an RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{K}
, a Hilbert space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions on
\mathcal{X}
where point evaluation is a continuous linear functional.
We denote the inner product and canonical norm of the RKHS by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=\sqrt{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}}
respectively.
The RKHS is also characterized by the following convenient

reproducing property
:
for each element
v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
,

v_{i}(x)=\langle v,\varphi_{i}(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}
,
where
v_{i}
is the
i
th component of
v
,

\varphi_{i}:x\mapsto K(\cdot,x)e_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{K}

with
e_{i}
the
i
th standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
1\leq i\leq d
.
For a comprehensive treatment of vector-valued RKHSs, we refer the reader to
Carmeli et al. 2006
.

Equipped with the necessary ingredients,
we define the KSD as follows, following
Barp et al. 2024
:

Definition 1

(Kernel Stein discrepancy)
.

Consider a target
P\in\mathcal{P}
with positive density
p\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
,
and a matrix-valued kernel
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

with RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{K}
satisfying the following conditions:

\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}({\cal H}_{K})=\{\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}v:v\in{\cal H}_{K}\}\subset L^{1}(P)
, and

\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},P)
.
We define the
kernel Stein discrepancy

\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}:\mathcal{P}\to[0,\infty]
by

\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q)\coloneqq\sup_{\lVert v\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1,\ (\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}v)^{+}\in L^{1}(Q)}\left|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}\left[{\cal T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}v(X)\right]\right|.

(2)

This KSD is also referred to as the
Langevin KSD
, as it is derived
from the Langevin Stein operator
{\cal T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}.

The requirements on
\mathcal{H}_{K}
in our definition guarantee the zero-mean property
\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}v]=0
for each
v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
For measures with finite moments, treated in the coming sections, we can enforce the integrability conditions in the definition by choosing a kernel of suitable growth (see
3.2
).
Note that
\mathcal{H}_{K}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
iff
(x,y)\mapsto\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(x,y)
is separately continuous and locally bounded for all
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}

(
Micheli and Glaunès 2014
, Theorem 2.11)
.
Moreover, the restriction
(\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}v)^{+}\in L^{1}(Q)
ensures that the KSD is well-defined on
\mathcal{P}
and takes values in
[0,\infty]
.

Computational tractability is an appeal of the KSD.
Indeed, the use of an RKHS yields a closed-form expression for the KSD.
To see this, let us define a scalar-valued kernel,
a Stein kernel

k_{p,K}
of
P
with base kernel
K,
by

k_{p,K}(x,y)=\frac{1}{p(x)p(y)}\left\langle\nabla_{y},\langle\nabla_{x},p(x)K(x,y)p(y)\rangle\right\rangle,\ x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(3)

where
\nabla_{x}
denotes applying the operator with respect to
x
.
Note that the Stein kernel can be computed even when
p
has an unknown normalizing constant.
The RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p,K}}
of the Stein kernel
k_{p,K}
coincides
with
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\mathrm{Id}}({\cal H}_{K})
,
and the KSD may be seen as
the maximum mean discrepancy
(MMD)
(
Gretton et al. 2012a
)
defined by
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p,K}}

(
Barp et al. 2024
, Theorem 1)
;
that is, the KSD is an IPM
(
Müller 1997
)
(with one argument fixed to
P
)

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}}(P,Q)\coloneqq\sup_{f\in{\cal F}:f^{+}\in L^{1}(Q)\ \text{or}\ f^{-}\in L^{1}(Q)}\left|\int f\differential P-\int f\differential Q\right|,

(4)

where
\mathcal{F}\subset L^{1}(P)
is specified to be the unit ball
\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{p,K}}:\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{p,K}}}\leq 1\}
of
\mathcal{H}_{k_{p,K}}
.
Then, the squared KSD is expressed by a double integral
(
Barp et al. 2024
, Corollary 1)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\int k_{p,K}(x,x^{\prime})\differential Q(x)\differential Q(x^{\prime}),

(5)

provided
{\cal H}_{k_{p,K}}\subset L^{1}(Q)
.
This expression is convenient particularly when
Q
is given as
a finitely-supported distribution
Q_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

with
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},

\delta_{x_{i}}

denoting the Dirac measure having unit mass at
x_{i},
and nonnegative
weights
w_{i}
such that
\sum_{i\leq n}w_{i}=1.
In this case,
we can compute the KSD exactly: the expression (
5
) with
Q=Q_{n}

results in

\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q_{n})^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}k_{p,K}(x_{i},x_{j}).

(6)

The measure
Q_{n}
may represent a weighted sample generated from a Monte Carlo algorithm, such as MCMC or sequential Monte Carlo
(
del Moral et al. 2006
)
.
When
Q_{n}
is the empirical measure formed by independent samples from
Q
,
the quantity
(
6
)
may alternatively be seen as the
V
-statistic
(
von Mises 1947
;
Serfling 2009
)
estimate of (
5
).
The KSD is thus a computable discrepancy measure; its computation only involves evaluating a target-dependent kernel on samples.

In this article, we deal with the
diffusion KSD
of
Barp et al. 2019

\mathrm{DKSD}_{K,P}(Q)=\sup_{\lVert v\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\leq 1,\ (\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v)^{+}\in L^{1}(Q)}\left|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}\left[{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v(X)\right]\right|,

which is defined by replacing the Langevin Stein operator in (
2
)
with the general diffusion Stein operator (
1
).
A benefit of a non-constant diffusion matrix
m
is that it allows us to provide convergence control guarantees for a broader class of targets, including heavy-tailed ones (see
Section

4.2
).
Nonetheless, the diffusion KSD may be treated as a specific instance of the
Langevin KSD:
Barp et al. 2019
shows that it
corresponds to the Langevin KSD defined by the kernel
K_{m}(x,y)=m(x)K(x,y)m(y)^{\top}

tilted by the diffusion matrix
m,
that is,
\mathrm{DKSD}_{K,P}(Q)=\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q).

This fact enables us to apply the theory for the Langevin KSD developed
by
Barp et al. 2024
, on which we build our main result (
3.1
).

3
KSD and
q
-Wasserstein convergence

We investigate the relationship between the KSD and
the quality of expectation approximation.
In particular, we are interested in how a decrease in KSD relates to an improvement in the quality of the approximation.
In this article, we focus on continuous integrands
f
of polynomial growth.
A natural starting point is to determine whether the KSD controls convergence in expectations of such functions.
This question may be formally stated as follows:
for a given sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of approximating probability measures,

\text{does convergence\ $\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}$($Q_{n}$) $\to$ 0\ }\text{imply}\ \int f\differential Q_{n}\to\int f\differential P\ \text{?}

The following sections present three results concerning this question.
In Section
3.1
, we formally define our target mode of convergence (
q
-Wasserstein convergence) and present a negative result:
standard KSDs, when applied with kernels previously recommended for weak convergence control, fall short of enforcing
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
This observation emphasizes the importance of kernel choice.
To overcome this limitation, we establish sufficient conditions on the approximation and growth properties of the kernel, demonstrating the equivalence between KSD- and
q
-Wasserstein convergence under these new conditions (
3.1
and
3.2
).
In Section
3.2
,
with additional assumptions, we obtain a rate of
q
-Wasserstein
convergence in terms of the KSD (
3.3
,
3.4
and
3.5
). With these two results, we establish
a theoretical foundation for the use of the KSD as a quality measure for distribution approximation.

3.1
KSD convergence and its relation to Wasserstein convergence

We begin this section by formalizing our task. For fixed
q\in[0,\infty),

define the class of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

with finite
q
th moments by

{\cal P}_{q}\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in{\cal P}:\int\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential\mu(x)<\infty\right\}.

We assume that our target
P
is an element of
{\cal P}_{q}
.
Our goal is to relate KSD convergence to
the following type of convergence:

Definition 2

(
q
-Wasserstein convergence and
q
-growth functions)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty)
. Suppose
P\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
For a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
the
q
-Wasserstein convergence
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
is defined as the convergence

\int\lvert f\rvert\differential Q_{n}\to\int\lvert f\rvert\differential P

holding true for any continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of

q
-growth
, i.e., such function satisfying the growth condition

\lvert f(x)\rvert\leq C\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\bigr)

everywhere for some
C>0.

Equivalently, the above definition states that
Q_{n}
eventually enters
{\cal P}_{q}
, and then

\int f\differential Q_{n}
is well-defined and converges to
\int f\differential P
, for each continuous
q
-growth function
f
.
Thus, it extends the standard notion of
weak convergence in
{\cal P}_{q}
to
\mathcal{P}
-valued sequences.
To avoid possible
confusion, we will reserve the
term
weak convergence
for the case
q=0,
i.e., convergence
with respect to all bounded continuous functions, and use the term

q
-Wasserstein convergence
for
q>0
.
This choice is made because the aforementioned type of convergence is also metrized by the
q
-Wasserstein distance
(
Villani 2003
, Theorems 7.3 and 7.12)
:

W_{q}(P,Q)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\left(\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(P,Q)}\mathbb{E}_{X,Y\sim\pi}\bigl[\lVert X-Y\rVert_{2}^{q}\bigr]\right)^{1\land(1/{q})}&\ \text{if}\ P,Q\in{\cal P}_{q},\\
\infty&\ \text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(7)

where
\Pi(P,Q)
denotes the set of all possible couplings of the probability measures
P
and
Q
.

To present our result, we make the following assumptions on the target density and the diffusion matrix:

Assumption 1

(Dissipativity)
.

Let
b(x)=\langle\nabla,p(x)m(x)\rangle/\{2p(x)\}.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
the target density
p
and the diffusion
matrix
m
satisfy

2\langle b(x),x\rangle+\mathrm{tr}\left[m(x)\right]\leq-\alpha\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}+\beta_{1}\lVert x\rVert_{2}+\beta_{0}

where
\mathrm{tr}\left[m(x)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}m_{ii}(x)

is the trace of
m(x),

\alpha>0,
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{0}\geq 0.

Assumption 2

(Growth)
.

Let
b(x)=\langle\nabla,p(x)m(x)\rangle/\{2p(x)\}.

The target density
p
and the diffusion matrix
m
satisfy
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\lambda_{b}\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr),\ \lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{m}\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{m}+1}\bigr),

where
c_{m}\geq 0,

\lambda_{b},\lambda_{m}>0,
and
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}.

In essence, these two assumptions determine the tail behavior of the target distribution.
To illustrate, suppose
m\equiv\mathrm{Id}
; we have
2b(x)=\nabla\log p(x)
in this case.
Then,
1
expresses that the target density has strongly log concave tails, akin to those of a Gaussian
(note that
2b(x)=-x
for the standard multivariate Gaussian density).
For example, a Gaussian mixture with shared covariance satisfies this condition
(
Gorham et al. 2019
, Example 3)
,
as well as the linear growth condition in
2
.
For distributions with tails heavier than Gaussian, choosing an appropriate coercive diffusion matrix
m
allows us to enforce
the dissipativity
1
.
We illustrate this point using a Student’s
t
-distribution in
Section

4.2
and

K.4
.
1
and
2
can also be verified with the right choice of
m
for a
multivariate Student’s
t
-regression posterior with a pseudo-Huber prior, as described by
Gorham et al. 2019
.

We will show that, for target distributions satisfying these assumptions, the KSD with a suitable kernel characterizes
uniform integrability
, defined as follows:

Definition 3

(Eventual uniform integrability of
q
th moments)
.

Let
q\in(0,\infty).
A sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}

is said to have
uniformly integrable
q
th moments
iff it

eventually
uniformly integrates
x\mapsto\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q},
i.e.,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q_{n}(x)=0.

It is known that for weakly converging sequences, the
q
-Wasserstein
convergence is equivalent to the uniform integrability of
q
th
moments
(
Villani 2003
, Theorem 7.12)
.
Intuitively, uniform integrability
is a condition enforcing that the probability mass does not diverge
too fast along the sequence. If
q=0,
the above definition
is reduced to uniform tightness
(see, e.g.,
Dudley 2002
, Chapter 9)
.
Uniform integrability is a stricter condition in that it requires
the decay rate of the tail probability to stay faster than a degree-
q

polynomial.
Note that in contrast to the standard definition in the literature
(see, e.g.,
Ambrosio et al. 2005
, Eq. 5.1.19)
,
our definition allows finitely many initial elements in the sequence to have infinite
q
th moments.

Under dissipativity assumptions as in
1
,
prior work
(
Gorham and Mackey 2017
;
Barp et al. 2024
)

identified conditions for the KSD to enforce tightness and weak convergence,
whereas uniform integrability was not established.
In fact, standard KSDs used for weak convergence control fall short of our purpose, as they can fail to enforce uniform integrability and hence to control
q
-Wasserstein convergence:

Proposition 3.1

(Standard KSDs fail to control moments)
.

Let
q\in(0,\infty)
and
P\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
Let
K
be a matrix-valued kernel satisfying the conditions in
1
.
Suppose

\sqrt{k_{p,K}(x,x)}=O(1+\lVert x\rVert^{q^{\prime}}_{2})

for some
q^{\prime}
with
0<q^{\prime}<q
.
Then,
\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q_{n})\to 0
does not imply
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
. Consequently, under
2
,
if both
K(x,x)
and
\{\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(x,x)\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
are bounded as functions of
x
,
the Langevin
\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(\cdot)
cannot control
q
-Wasserstein convergence for
q>1
.

The proof can be found in

Appendix

A
.
Intuitively, this limitation arises from the lack of
q
-growth functions in the Stein RKHS.
Indeed, under
2
, the Stein kernel cannot have super-linear growth with a bounded base kernel, and hence the Langevin KSD is unable to control
q
th moment convergence for
q>1
.
This negative result applies to the most common base kernels used with KSDs,
including the IMQ and log-inverse kernels recommended by
Gorham and Mackey 2017
and
Chen et al. 2018
, respectively; it also encompasses the B-spline, Gaussian, Matérn, sech, sinc, and Wendland’s compactly supported kernels.
Moreover, our experiment in
Section

4.1
demonstrates the inability of the IMQ-KSD to detect non-convergence in the second moment in practice.
This observation motivates us to seek conditions under which the KSD controls both
q
th
moment uniform integrability and weak convergence.
In the following, we first provide sufficient conditions for enforcing uniform integrability.
Then, building on the weak convergence result of
Barp et al. 2024
,
we prove that the resulting KSD controls
q
-Wasserstein convergence.

A sufficient condition for enforcing
q
th moment uniform
integrability is given as follows:

Definition 4

(
q
-growth approximation)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty).
A set

{\cal F}
of real-valued functions is said to
approximate
q
-growth

if, for each
\varepsilon>0,
there exists
r_{\varepsilon}>0
and
a function
f_{\varepsilon}\in{\cal F}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

f_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon}\}-\varepsilon.

(8)

We also say
\mathcal{F}
approximates
q
-growth with respect to
P

if
\mathcal{F}\subset L^{1}(P)
and its
P
-centered version
\mathcal{F}^{P}=\{f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
approximates
q
-growth.

Definition
4
states that using an element of
{\cal F}
, we can either dominate or accurately approximate from below a function that is zero on a ball and grows like
\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}
outside of that ball.
When
\mathcal{F}
is
P
-centered, the case
q=0
corresponds to the
indicator dominating property of
Barp et al. 2024
,
which is used to enforce tightness with an IPM.

3.1
below shows that an IPM can enforce uniform integrability if the defining function class approximates
q
-growth, which readily yields the conclusion for the KSD (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.1

(Controlling UI with growth approximation)
.

Fix
q\in[0,\infty)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a real vector space of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

\lambda:\mathcal{F}\to[0,\infty)
be a function satisfying
\lambda(cf)=c\lambda(f)
for any
c\in(0,\infty)
.
If
\mathcal{F}
approximates
q
-growth with respect to
P\in{\cal P}_{q}
,
then, a sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P}
has uniformly integrable
q
th moments
if
d_{\mathcal{B}}(P,Q_{n})\to 0
, where
d_{\mathcal{B}}
is the IPM (
4
) defined by
\mathcal{B}=\{f\in\mathcal{F}:\lambda(f)\leq 1\}
.

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon>0,
by the
q
-growth approximation
property (
8
) of
\mathcal{F}^{P}
, there exists
f_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{F}

and
r_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\int_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon}\}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q_{n}(x)\leq\int f_{\varepsilon}\differential Q_{n}-\int f_{\varepsilon}\differential P+\varepsilon\leq\{1\lor\lambda(f_{\varepsilon})\}d_{\mathcal{B}}(P,Q_{n})+\varepsilon.

Taking the limit
n\to\infty
concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.1

(Enforcing UI with KSD)
.

Suppose
P
and
K
satisfy the conditions in
1
.
Assume that the Stein RKHS
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}({\cal H}_{K})
approximates
q
-growth.
Then,
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
has uniformly integrable
q
th moments
if
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

We thus explore sufficient conditions on the reproducing kernel to guarantee
q
-growth approximation and weak convergence control.
In what follows, we show that it suffices to use a kernel of the following form:

Definition 5

(Basic kernel form)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty)
.
Let
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

be a matrix-valued kernel defined by

K(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)\left(L(x,y)+\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)\mathrm{Id}\right)w_{q-1}(y),

(9)

where, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
w_{s}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{s/2}
with
\tau>0
,

L
is a matrix-valued kernel with
\mathcal{H}_{L}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and

\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)=\frac{k_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)}{\sqrt{k_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,x)}\sqrt{k_{\mathrm{lin}}(y,y)}}

is the normalized version of a linear kernel
k_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle+\tau^{2}
with bias
\tau^{2}>0
.

Our next lemma identifies concrete kernels that realize the
q
-growth approximation
property.
To establish this result, we will rely on the concept of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universality
(
Simon-Gabriel and Schölkopf 2018
, Definition 5)
,
which states that each element of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, up to its first derivatives, can be approximated uniformly over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by an element of the RKHS; that is, the RKHS is dense in
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Lemma 3.2

(Kernel choice for
q
-growth approximation)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty).

Define
K
as in
5
.
Assume the dissipativity condition in Assumption
1

with
\alpha>0
.
Assume the growth conditions in Assumption
2
.
If
q_{m}=0
in
2
,
assume that
\mathcal{H}_{L}
is universal to
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
If
q_{m}=1
,
assume the following conditions:
(a)
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2),

(b)
L=\ell\mathrm{Id}
where
\ell
is a scalar-valued
kernel such that
\ell(x,y)=\Phi(x-y)/\{w_{1}(x)w_{1}(y)\}
for some
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
;
and (c)
\Phi
has a continuous non-vanishing generalized Fourier transform (see our definition in
Section

1
).
Then,
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
approximates
q
-growth.

Our proof in
Section

B.2
relies on
1
and
2
to construct a function to approximate
q
-growth. As this may not be in the RKHS, we further approximate it using an element of the RKHS, which is possible due to our kernel choice, particularly the universal kernel
L
.

Having derived a kernel choice for the KSD to enforce uniform integrability,
we now present a result that addresses the question posed at the outset:

Theorem 3.1

(Vanishing KSD implies
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

Let
q\in(0,\infty)
and
P\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
Define a base kernel
K
as in
5
. Suppose that diffusion matrix satisfies the following conditions:
(a)
m(x)
is invertible for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\lVert m^{-1}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O(1)
,
and
(b)
\lVert\langle\nabla,m(x)\rangle\rVert_{2}=O(\lVert x\rVert_{2})
.
Assume the dissipativity condition in
1
with
\alpha>0
.
Assume the growth conditions in
2
with
\lambda_{m}>0
and
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
.
Define

\displaystyle L^{(1)}(x,y)=\frac{L_{0}(x,y)}{{w}_{q+1}(x){w}_{q+1}(y)},\ L^{(2)}(x,y)=\frac{\ell(x,y)}{w_{q_{m}}(x)w_{q_{m}}(y)}\cdot\mathrm{Id},

where

L_{0}
is universal to
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;
and
\ell
is a scalar-valued translation-invariant kernel universal to
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}
such that

\ell(x,y)=\Phi(x-y)
for some
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
.
If
q_{m}=1,

additionally, assume the following:
(a)
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2),

and (b)
\Phi
has continuous non-vanishing generalized Fourier transform.
Specify kernel
L
in
K
to be either of
L^{(1)}
or
L^{(2)}
if
q_{m}=0
,
and to be
L^{(2)}
if
q_{m}=1
.
Then, for any sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
we have
Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
if
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof sketch
.

We outline our proof, which can be found in

Section

C.1
. Our choices of
K
and
m
ensure that
the Stein RKHS
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
approximates
q
-growth (by
3.2
) and hence that the KSD enforces
q
-uniform integrability (by
3.1
).
Moreover, each choice of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universal kernel
L
ensures that the KSD controls weak convergence for tight sequences.
Specifically, the tilting by
w_{q+1}
in
L^{(1)}

and the use of translation-invariant kernel in
L^{(2)}
guarantee that
the Stein RKHS admits a subset separating
P
from arbitrary probability measures, which implies the required control of weak convergence
(
Barp et al. 2024
, Theorems 6-9)
.

Remark 1
.

If
q_{m}=1
, without the tilting in
L^{(2)}
in
3.1
,
the Stein RKHS
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
may contain functions of
(q+1)
-growth due to the quadratic growth of
m
.
Nonetheless,
3.1
holds with
L^{(3)}(x,y)=\ell(x,y)\mathrm{Id}
substituted for
L^{(2)}
provided that
P\in{\cal P}_{q+1}
.

Remark 2
.

In Theorem
3.1

(see also Lemma
3.2
),
the quadratic growth (
q_{m}=1
) of
\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
requires

\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2),
which limits the
allowed growth
q
of test functions. This condition may
be relaxed to
\alpha>\lambda_{m}\{q+\min(2,\theta-2)\}

if
P\in{\cal P}_{q+\theta}
for some
\theta>0
by choosing the tilting function
w_{q+\theta-1}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q+\theta-1)/2}
in the definition of
K
.
Our argument relies on the existence of a
coercive function
,
with growth rate
\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q+\theta}
as
\left\|x\right\|_{2}\to\infty
. Prior work
(
Gorham and Mackey 2017
;
Chen et al. 2018
;
Huggins and Mackey 2018
;
Hodgkinson et al. 2020
)
used a
coercive function to ensure that the KSD enforces uniform tightness

(see also
Barp et al. 2024
, Lemma 1)
.
We defer the proof of this remark to

Appendix

D
.

Our next results, proved in

Section

C.2
, show that, under mild conditions,
q
-Wasserstein convergence implies KSD convergence to zero.

Proposition 3.2

(KSD detects
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

For a target
P
and a base kernel
K
, suppose the Stein kernel
k_{p,K}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous with
\sqrt{k_{p,K}(x,x)}=O(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q})

for some
q\in(0,\infty)
.
Then, for any sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
we have
\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q_{n})\to 0
if

Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.

Corollary 3.2

(User-friendly conditions for detecting
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

For a target
P
and diffusion matrix
m
, suppose
2
holds with growth exponent
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
.
If, for a base kernel
K
, some
q\in(0,\infty)
, and each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
,
\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(x,y)
is continuous and

\lVert K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O\left(\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)^{q-1}\right),\ \lVert\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O\left(\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)^{q-q_{m}-1}\right)

then the preconditions of
3.2
hold for the Stein kernel
k_{p,K_{m}}
.

Taken together,
3.1
and
3.2
provide broad sufficient conditions under which KSD convergence is exactly equivalent to
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
The proof of this corollary can be found in

Section

C.2.3
.

Corollary 3.3

(KSD-Wasserstein equivalence)
.

Instantiate the assumptions and notation of
3.1
, and assume
\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}L_{0}
is continuous for each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
.
Then, for any sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
we have
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
if and only if

Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.

Note that
3.1
and
3.2
build on different sets of assumptions.
Effectively,
3.2
only requires the continuous differentiablility and a suitable growth of the reproducing kernel,
and unlike in
3.1
, the corresponding Stein RKHS need not separate all distributions from the target.
The kernel
L^{(1)}
in
3.1
offers great generality but might be too abstract for applications.
The choice
L^{(2)}
is substantially simpler and includes a broad class of kernels used in practice including Matérn (wih smoothness greater than 1), Gaussian, and IMQ kernels.
For ease of application, we therefore provide a simplified form of
3.3
based on an IMQ kernel (see

Section

C.2.4
for a proof).

Corollary 3.4

(User-friendly KSD-Wasserstein equivalence)
.

Fix
q\in(1,\infty)
.
For a target
P
and a diffusion matrix
m
, suppose that

1
and
2
hold with

\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2)
if
q_{m}=1
,

m(x)
invertible for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\lVert m^{-1}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O(1)
,
and

\lVert\langle\nabla,m(x)\rangle\rVert_{2}=O(\lVert x\rVert_{2})
.
Let
K=k_{\textup{IMQ${}^{+}$}}\mathrm{Id}
for a scalar-valued kernel
k_{\textup{IMQ${}^{+}$}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

k_{\textup{IMQ${}^{+}$}}(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)\left(\frac{k_{\textup{IMQ}}(x,y)}{w_{q_{m}}(x)w_{q_{m}}(y)}+\frac{1+\langle x,y\rangle}{w_{1}(x)w_{1}(y)}\right)w_{q-1}(y)

where
k_{\textup{IMQ}}(x,y)=\bigl(1+\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{-1/2}
,
and

\tau=1
for the tilting
w_{\bullet}
.
Then, for any sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
we have
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
if and only if

Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.

The informativeness of the KSD will depend on the choice of the kernel, particularly in high dimensions.
We recommend the above as a default choice but not as a universal solution.
A more refined approach would be to modify the kernel according to application, e.g., by introducing a scaling parameter to the input of
k_{\textup{IMQ}}
,
using a different bias parameter
\tau^{2}
when tilting with
w_{\bullet}
, or introducing a location parameter in evaluating the norm
\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}
and the linear inner product.
Kernel selection is an ongoing research topic and beyond the scope of this article.
Nonetheless, as an empirical study, we discuss a heuristic kernel selection method from goodness-of-fit testing in
Section

4.3
.

We close this section with a brief discussion of related work.

Modeste and Dombry 2023
derived sufficient conditions for an MMD to metrize
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
Recall that the KSD is a specific MMD defined by a Stein kernel (see
Section

2
and

Appendix

A
).
However, the results of
Modeste and Dombry 2023
do not apply to KSDs, as they consider specific classes of kernels that exclude Stein kernels, namely energy kernels (the covariance functions of fractional Brownian motions) and sums of translation-invariant kernels and moment-characterizing kernels.
To the best of our knowledge, our work establishes the first KSD-Wasserstein equivalence results.

3.2
Rate of
q
-Wasserstein convergence via a KSD bound

The topological equivalence established in
Section

3.1
does not express the quantitative relation between the two modes of convergence.
In this section, we clarify this connection by relating the KSD to
an IPM that metrizes
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
Specifically, we obtain an explicit KSD upper bound on the IPM, thereby demonstrating the speed of
q
-Wasserstein convergence relative to KSD convergence.

Before presenting our results, it is instructive to outline our strategy.
Our approach here is an adaptation of Stein’s method
(
Stein 1972
;
Ross 2011
)
.
For a given
q
-growth function
f
in a (sufficiently
regular) function class
\mathcal{F},
consider the
Stein equation

{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v=f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f],

(10)

where the equality is pointwise. If we assume the existence of a solution

v_{f}
to (
10
) for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we can then obtain a bound on the
worst-case expectation error with respect to
{\cal F}:

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}}(P,Q)\leq\sup_{f\in{\cal F}}\left|\int{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}\differential Q\right|,

where
Q\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
Our goal is to express the upper bound in terms of the KSD. To this
end, we approximate each
v_{f}
by an RKHS function
\tilde{v}_{f}\in{\cal H}_{K}
,
which yields an estimate

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in{\cal F}}\left|\int\bigl({\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}-{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\tilde{v}_{f}\bigr)\differential Q\right|+\sup_{f\in{\cal F}}\lVert\tilde{v}_{f}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\cdot\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q).

(11)

Evaluating this upper bound requires assessing the approximation error
and the norm of the approximation; this task calls for regularity
properties of the approximand
v_{f}
and a characterization of the complexity
of the RKHS.

In the following, we first introduce a function class
{\cal F}

that both provides a differentiable solution to the Stein equation
and induces an IPM
d_{{\cal F}}
metrizing
q
-Wasserstein
convergence. We then specify a kernel function to obtain a concrete
bound.

3.2.1
Pseudo-Lipschitz functions

We first specify the function class
{\cal F}
. In our analysis,
we consider a set
{\cal F}_{q}
of
pseudo-Lipschitz

functions of order
q-1
defined by

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{q}\coloneqq\big\{f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}:\

\displaystyle f\in{\cal C}^{3}\ \text{with}\ \left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},q-1}\leq 1

\displaystyle\text{and}\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\nabla^{i}f(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}/\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}\bigr)\leq 1\ \text{for }i\in\{2,3\}\big\},

where

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},q-1}=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\bigl\{(1+1\{q>1\}(\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}+\lVert y\rVert_{2}^{q-1})\bigr\}\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}},\ q\geq 1.

Pseudo-Lipschitz functions of order
q-1
are defined as
those
f
with
\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},q-1}<\infty.
The seminorm

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},q-1}
generalizes the Lipschitz seminorm

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},0},
and pseudo-Lipschitz functions are allowed to have

q
-growth.
Indeed,

Appendix

E
shows that
{\cal F}_{q}
contains degree-
q

monomial functions of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;
in particular, if
f(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{q_{i}}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}=q
,
we have

\lvert\mathbb{E}_{P}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{Q}[f]\rvert\leq c_{q,d}d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)
,
where
c_{q,d}
is a constant given in

E.1
. Thus, any quantative bound on
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
translates directly into the
q
th moment approximation error, justifying our choice of the function class
\mathcal{F}_{q}
.

The corresponding IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
is indeed a metric on
{\cal P}_{q}
and characterizes
q
-Wasserstein
convergence.

Proposition 3.3

(Pseudo-Lipschitz metric)
.

For fixed
q\in[1,\infty)
,
assume
P\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
For any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P},

the convergence
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,P_{n})\to 0

is equivalent to
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.

Proof sketch
.

We consider the set
\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(\supset\mathcal{F}_{q})
of
1
-pseudo-Lipschitz functions and prove that the corresponding IPM
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}}
metrizes
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
For one direction,
we show that
it is sufficiently large to enforce uniform integrability and to control weak convergence via the bounded-Lipschitz metric
(see, e.g.,
Dudley 2002
, Section 11.2)
.
For the other, we show that
q
-Wasserstein convergence is sufficient to control the pseudo-Lipschitz class
\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}
. The claim about
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
then follows from the equivalence between
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
and
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}}
in characterizing convergence to
P
.
See

Section

F.1
for a complete proof.

3.2.2
Characterization of a solution to the Stein equation

Erdogdu et al. 2018
studied a solution to the Stein equation defined
by the generator of an Itô diffusion process and a pseudo-Lipschitz
function. Building on their result, we can obtain a solution with
known regularity properties as in
Gorham et al. 2019
.

The diffusion Stein operator was derived by
Gorham et al. 2019

from the infinitesimal generators of Itô diffusion processes. Here,
let us assume that the diffusion matrix
m
is given by
m(x)=a(x)+c(x),

where
a(x)
is called a covariance coefficient and expressed as

a(x)=\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top}
by a diffusion coefficient
\sigma(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

and
c(x)
a stream coefficient satisfying
c(x)=-c(x)^{\top}.

Consider the diffusion defined by the stochastic differential equation

\differential Z_{t}^{x}=b(Z_{t}^{x})\differential t+\sigma(Z_{t}^{x})\differential B_{t}\ \text{with }Z_{0}^{x}=x,

(12)

where
(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a
d
-dimensional Wiener process.
In the following, we additionally assume
b
and
\sigma
are locally Lipschitz to guarantee a unique solution to (
12
)
(
Ethier and Kurtz 1986
, Theorem 3.11, p.300)
.
Then, the diffusion Stein operator (
1
) is related
to the generator
\mathcal{A}_{P}
of the diffusion by

{\cal A}_{P}u(x)=\frac{1}{2}{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\nabla u(x),

where
u:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.
Consequently, if we obtain a solution

u_{f}
to the
Poisson equation

{\cal A}_{P}u=f-\mathbb{E}_{P}[f],\ f\in{\cal F}_{q},

(13)

we then have a solution to the Stein equation (
10
)
by taking
v_{f}=2^{-1}\nabla u_{f}.

Erdogdu et al. 2018
proved that the Poisson equation (
13
)
admits a suitably regular solution
u_{f}
under the following additional assumptions on the diffusion:

Assumption 3

(Dissipativity)
.

For
\alpha,\beta>0,
the
diffusion (
12
) satisfies the dissipativity condition

\mathcal{A}_{P}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq-\alpha\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}+\beta,

where
\mathcal{A}_{P}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}=2\left\langle b(x),x\right\rangle+\lVert\sigma(x)\rVert_{F}^{2}.

Assumption 4

(Wasserstein rate)
.

For
s\in\{1,2\}
,
the diffusion
Z_{t}^{x}
and
s
-Wasserstein distance (
7
) admit an
L^{s}
-Wasserstein rate
function

\rho_{s}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

satisfying

W_{s}\bigl(\mathrm{Law}(Z_{t}^{x}),\mathrm{Law}(Z_{t}^{y}))\bigr)\leq\rho_{s}(t)\lVert x-y\rVert_{2},\ \text{for all}\ x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{and}\ t\geq 0.

Define the following relative
L^{1}
- and
L^{2}
-Wasserstein
rates:

\tilde{\rho}_{1}(t)=\log\frac{\rho_{2}(t)}{\rho_{1}(t)}\ \text{and}\ \tilde{\rho}_{2}(t)=\frac{\log\{\rho_{1}(t)/\rho_{1}(0)\}-\log\rho_{2}(t)}{\log\{\rho_{1}(t)/\rho_{1}(0)\}}.

Let
q\in[1,\infty).
Assume further that
L^{1}
- and
L^{2}
-Wasserstein rates satisfy

\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{1}(t)\left\{1+\rho_{1}(t)^{1-1/(q_{m}+1)}\left(\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{q_{m}+1}(t)}{\tilde{\alpha}_{q_{m}+1}}\right)^{q-1}\right\}\differential t<\infty,

where

\tilde{\alpha}_{1}=\alpha,\ \text{and}\ \tilde{\alpha}_{2}=\inf_{t\geq 0}\left[\alpha-4\lambda_{m}(q-1)\bigl(1\lor\tilde{\rho}_{2}(t)\bigr)\right]_{+},

with
\alpha
and
\lambda_{m}
from
3
.

Under these assumptions,
Erdogdu et al. 2018
show that
the solution
u_{f}
is pseudo-Lipschitz of order
q-1

and has
(q-1)
-growth continuous derivatives up to the third
order
(see
J.1
in
Appendix

J
for a complete statement). Based on this result, we obtain the following
characterization of the solution
v_{f}=2^{-1}\nabla u_{f}
.

Lemma 3.3

(Adaptation of
Erdogdu et al. 2018
)
.

Assume
f\in{\cal F}_{q}
.
Under Assumptions
2
,
3
,
and
4
with
s=1,
there exists a
solution
v_{f}
to the Stein equation (
10
). Moreover,
there exist positive constants
\zeta_{1}(P),\zeta_{2}(P),\zeta_{3}(P)

(called Stein factors) independent of
f
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\lVert v_{f}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\zeta_{1}(P)(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}),

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla^{i}v_{f}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{\zeta_{i}(P)}{2}(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1})\ \text{for }i\in\{1,2\}.

Assumption
3
is a special
case of Assumption
1
.
Although
1
also implies
3
, the constants
\alpha
appearing in these assumptions
do not agree. We use the formulation of Assumption

3
to more closely mimic the presentation of
Erdogdu et al. 2018
. Assumption
4
essentially requires
the diffusion to have an integrable
L^{1}
-Wasserstein rate,
while allowing the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein rate to grow (e.g., we may
have
\rho_{1}(t)=e^{-c_{1}t}
and
\rho_{2}(t)=e^{c_{2}t}
with
c_{1},c_{2}>0
). If

q_{m}=1,
the assumption also requires
\alpha>4\lambda_{m}(q-1)

to ensure
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}>0.
This condition trivially holds
if
q=1;
otherwise, it may be stronger than the condition

\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2)
assumed in Lemma
3.2
.
There are two known sufficient conditions for establishing exponential
Wasserstein decay. The first is uniform dissipativity, which is a
simple (but more restrictive) condition leading to exponential
L^{1}
-
or
L^{2}
- exponential decay rates.

Proposition 3.4

(Wasserstein decay from uniform dissipativity,
Wang 2020
)
.

A diffusion with drift
and diffusion coefficients
b
and
\sigma
has
L^{s}
-Wasserstein
rate
\rho_{s}(t)=e^{-rt/2},
if for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

the following uniform dissipativity holds:

2\langle b(x)-b(y),x-y\rangle+\lVert\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+(s-2)\lVert\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq-r\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}^{2}.

The second and more general condition is distant dissipativity. Explicit

L^{1}
-Wasserstein decay rates from distant dissipativity are obtained
by the following result of
Gorham et al. 2019
, which builds
upon the analyses of
Eberle 2015
and
Wang 2020
.

Proposition 3.5

(Wasserstein decay from distant dissipativity,
Gorham et al. 2019
)
.

A diffusion with drift and diffusion
coefficients
b
and
\sigma
is called distantly dissipative if
for the truncated diffusion coefficient

\tilde{\sigma}(x)\coloneqq(\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top}-s^{2}\mathrm{Id})^{1/2}\ \text{with}\ s\in[0,1/M_{0}(\sigma^{-1}))

with
M_{0}(\sigma^{-1})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\sigma^{-1}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

it satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{2\langle b(x)-b(y),x-y\rangle}{s^{2}\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\tilde{\sigma}(x)-\tilde{\sigma}(y)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}{s^{2}\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\lVert\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}(x)-\tilde{\sigma}(y)\bigr)^{\top}(x-y)\rVert_{2}^{2}}{s^{2}\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}-U&\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}>R\\
L&\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}\leq R\end{cases}

for some
U>0
and
R,L\geq 0.
If the distant dissipativity holds,
then the diffusion has Wasserstein rate
\rho_{1}(t)=2e^{LR^{2}/8}e^{-rt/2}

for

s^{2}r^{-1}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{e-1}{2}R^{2}+e\sqrt{8U^{-1}}R+4U^{-1}&\text{if}\ LR^{2}\leq 8,\\
8\sqrt{2\pi}R^{-1}L^{-1/2}(L^{-1}+U^{-1})\exp\Bigl(\frac{LR^{2}}{8}\Bigr)+32R^{-2}U^{-2}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

These two conditions also conveniently lead to the dissipativity condition
(Assumption
1
) defined above.

3.2.3
Explicit bound on the pseudo-Lipschitz metric

The previous sections set the stage for our main result, presented in this section. Our first result below relates the IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}

and the KSD.

Theorem 3.2

(KSD bound on
d_{{\cal\mathcal{F}}_{q}}
; an informal statement
1
1

1

A precise but less easily interpretable bound is available in
G.1
.

)
.

Fix
q\in[1,\infty)
.
Let
K=k\mathrm{Id}
with a scalar-valued kernel
k
.
Following the notation in
5
, define
k
as follows:

k(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)\left(\frac{\Phi(x-y)}{w_{q_{m}}(x)w_{q_{m}}(y)}+\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)\right)w_{q-1}(y),

where

\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
is a positive definite function with continuous generalized Fourier transform
\hat{\Phi}
,

q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
is the growth exponent of
\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
(
2
).
Then, under
2
and
3
,

4
, for any
Q\in{\cal P}

and
\varepsilon,\rho>0,
we have

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}_{q}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle\leq C_{P,d}\left\{\rho\cdot r_{\varepsilon}^{q+q_{m}}+r_{\varepsilon}^{q_{m}+d/2}\left(\sqrt{\sup_{\lVert\omega\rVert_{2}\leq 2(\rho_{\varepsilon}\land\rho)^{-1}}\hat{\Phi}(\omega)^{-1}}\cdot\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)\right)+\varepsilon\right\},

where
r_{\varepsilon}
and
\rho_{\varepsilon}
are increasing
functions of
\varepsilon^{-1}
and
C_{P,d}
is an explicit constant specified in

G.1
.

Proof.

We provide a proof sketch (see

Appendix

G

for a complete proof).
From (
11
), for any
r>0
and
\tilde{v}_{f}\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)&\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{q}}\int_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r}\lvert{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}-{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\tilde{v}_{f}\rvert\differential Q+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{q}}\int_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r}\left(\lvert{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}\rvert+\lvert{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\tilde{v}_{f}\rvert\right)\differential Q\\
&\hphantom{\leadsto}\quad+\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{q}}\lVert\tilde{v}_{f}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\cdot\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q).\end{aligned}

(14)

Each term is evaluated as follows. We construct the approximation
\tilde{v}_{f}

by mollifying the solution
v_{f}
to the Stein equation with a Fourier
multiplier. This makes the first term small, since
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\tilde{v}_{f}

can approximate the target
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}
uniformly inside
the ball of radius
r
up to precision
\rho.
Under the stated assumptions,
we have that both
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v_{f}
and
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\tilde{v}_{f}

are of
q
-growth, and there exists a function
v_{\varepsilon}
in
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}({\cal H}_{K})

that can approximate
\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}
for sufficiently
large
r=r_{\varepsilon}
. Thus, the second term can be bounded by

\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)+\varepsilon
.
The norms
\lVert\tilde{v}_{f}\rVert_{{\cal H}_{K}}
and
\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{{\cal H}_{K}}

can be evaluated
using
Wendland 2004
, which expresses the norm
in terms of Fourier transforms of
\tilde{v}_{f}
and
\Phi;
this
norm is upper-bounded uniformly over
\mathcal{F}_{q}
, as all functions in
{\cal F}_{q}

have uniformly bounded
q
- and
(q-1)
-growth coefficients for their
values and their derivatives, respectively.
∎

Remark 3
.

Gorham and Mackey 2017
established a bound on the bounded-Lipschitz metric,
to which our proof similarly applies. In essence, their argument relies
on the existence of a coercive function to obtain a KSD bound on the
second term of the RHS in (
14
). Our
proof uses the less strict
q
-growth approximation property. More specifically,
Gorham and Mackey 2017
uses
a distribution-dependent quantity called a
tightness rate
to
determine the radius
r
in (
14
), whereas
our choice of
r
is distribution-independent. While our result is more broadly applicable, the assumption of a higher-order moment can yield an even tighter bound.
In

Appendix

D
, we generalize the coercivity strategy of
Gorham and Mackey 2017
to obtain a similar quantity specifying the radius
r
. This in turn can be used
to enforce
q
th moment uniform integrability.

Once
\Phi
is specified, Theorem
3.2

enables us to trade off the complexity of the approximation and its
precision. We carry out this task below; the following two results
present concrete bounds for the two growth cases of diffusion matrix
m
:
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
.
The proofs of these theorems are deferred to

Sections

G.3.4
and
G.3.5
.

Theorem 3.3

(Matérn KSD bound for the linear growth case)
.

Fix
q\in[1,\infty)
.
Define kernel
K=k\mathrm{Id}
as in Theorem
3.2

with
\Phi
specified by the Matérn kernel with smoothness parameter

\nu>1

\Phi_{\mathrm{Mat}}(x)=\frac{2^{1-(d/2+\nu)}}{\Gamma(d/2+\nu)}\left\|\Sigma x\right\|_{2}^{\nu}K_{-\nu}\bigl(\lVert\Sigma x\rVert_{2}\bigr),

(15)

where

\Gamma
is the Gamma function,

\Sigma
a strictly positive definite matrix,
and
K_{-\nu}
the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order
-\nu
.
Suppose Assumption
2

holds with
q_{m}=0.

Suppose that
3
and
4
hold.
Then, there exists an explicit
constant
A_{P,d}>0

(given in
Section

G.3.4
) such that, for any
Q\in{\cal P}
,

d_{{\cal F}_{q}}(P,Q)\leq A_{P,d}\Bigl(1\lor\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{{}^{\frac{t_{0}}{1+t_{0}}\lor\frac{q(q/3+1)}{1+t_{0}}}}\Bigr)\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{\frac{1}{1+t_{0}}}

where
t_{0}=(1+q/6)d+\nu(q+5)/3.

Theorem 3.4

(Matérn KSD bound for the quadratic growth case)
.

Define kernel
K=k\mathrm{Id}
as in Theorem
3.2
using the Matérn kernel (
15
).
Suppose Assumption
2
holds with
q_{m}=1.

Suppose that
3
and
4
hold.
Then,
there exists an explicit constant
B_{P,d}>0

(given in
Section

G.3.5
) such that, for any
Q\in{\cal P}
,

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}_{q}}(P,Q)\leq B_{P,d}\Bigl(1\lor\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{\frac{t_{1}+1/3}{1+t_{1}}\lor\frac{(q^{2}+3q-2)}{3(1+t_{1})}}\Bigr)\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{1+t_{1}}}

with

t_{1}=\frac{\{(q+1)(1+d^{-1})+6\}d}{6}+\frac{\{(q+1)(1+d^{-1})+5\}\nu+1}{3}.

The dimension dependence in
3.3
and
3.4
reflects the difficulty of controlling the
IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
in high dimensions.
To see this, consider the case

q=1
and an approximation of
P
by the empirical measure
Q_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}

with independent sample points
X_{i}\sim P
.

Mackey and Gorham 2016
established the following relationship between the IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
and the
1
-Wasserstein distance (with
G
a standard normal vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
):

\textstyle\min\big(\frac{1}{3}W_{1}(P,Q),\frac{1}{27\sqrt{2}\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}]^{2}}W_{1}(P,Q)^{3}\big)\leq d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}(P,Q).

(16)

Moreover,
Dudley 1969
showed that
\mathbb{E}[W_{1}(P,Q_{n})]=\Omega(n^{-1/d})
whenever
P
has a Lebesgue density.
In contrast, the KSD is relatively easy to decrease:
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})]=O(n^{-1/2})
whenever
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})^{2}]<\infty

(
Serfling 2009
, p.194)
.
With this rate substituted, our
3.3
and
3.4
upper
bounds imply roughly

n^{-6/(7d)}
and
n^{-1/(2d)}
rates of decay for
\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}(P,Q_{n})]
.
While this dimension dependence may not be optimal, it reasonably illustrates
the relation between the two quantities and reflects the challenge of decreasing

d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
in
high dimensions.
We suspect that a tighter relationship can be deduced between the KSD and a less stringent IPM with a smaller test function class
than
\mathcal{F}_{q}
.
This would require a new analysis
of the Stein equation and its solutions over the IPM’s test function class, and we leave such refinements for future
work.

One might also be interested in a direct comparison between the KSD and the
q
-Wasserstein distance
W_{q}
(
7
).
For
q=1
, the relation (
16
) allows us to translate our bounds on
d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
into KSD bounds directly on
W_{1}
.
Moreover, our next result, proved in

Section

H.1
, provides direct control of
W_{q}
under the assumptions of
3.3
or
3.4
.

Theorem 3.5

(KSD bounds on
W_{q}
)
.

Fix
q\in(1,\infty)
and
assume

P\in{\cal P}_{q}
.
Instantiate the assumptions and notation of
3.3
with
q_{m}=0
or
3.4
with
q_{m}=1
.
Then
there exists an explicit constant
C_{P,d}(q_{m})
, given in

Section

H.1
, such that, for any
Q\in{\cal P}
,

\displaystyle W_{q}(P,Q)\leq C_{P,d}(q_{m})\left(1\lor\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{1-\gamma(q_{m})}\right)\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)^{\gamma(q_{m})}

where

\displaystyle\gamma(q_{m})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{q(q+2)}\cdot\frac{1}{1+t_{0}}&\text{if}\ q_{m}=0,\\
\frac{1}{q\{(1+d^{-1})q+2-d^{-1}\}}\cdot\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{1+t_{1}}&\text{if}\ q_{m}=1,\end{cases}

where
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
are constants from
3.3
and
3.4
.

Finally, we mention related work by
Vayer and Gribonval 2023
, which provides MMD bounds on
W_{q}
with a similar dependence on dimension
d
.
Although the KSD is an instance of MMD, their results do not directly lead to a KSD bound, as they require the MMD to be defined by the RKHS of a translation-invariant kernel, which the Stein kernel does not satisfy.

4
Numerical illustration

We conduct numerical experiments to examine the theory developed above.
In the first two experiments, we investigate how kernel choice affects
the KSD’s ability to detect non-convergence in moments, using simple
light-tailed and heavy-tailed target distributions. We then present
a cautionary case study, in which, even though the KSD will asymptotically
detect moment discrepancies by Theorem
3.1
,
a large sample size is needed for the KSD to detect discrepancies
arising from isolated modes.

4.1
Detecting second moment non-convergence

3.1
implies that the standard Langevin KSD with IMQ base kernel—often recommended for weak convergence control—can fail to detect non-convergence of second moments (corresponding to the case
q=2
).
Here we provide a concrete example verifying this failure mode.

We construct a simple sequence that does not converge to its target in
the second moment.
We use the
d
-dimensional standard Gaussian distribution
{\cal N}(\mathbf{0},\mathrm{Id})
as a target
P
with
d=5
.
We then choose an approximating sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}

as follows:

Q_{n}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n+1}\right)P_{n}+\frac{1}{n+1}\delta_{x_{n}}\ \text{ with }\ P_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{j}},

(17)

where
x_{n}=\sqrt{n+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}
with
\mathbf{1}=(1,\dots,1)
, and
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P
.
By
Dudley 2002
, the sequence
Q_{n}
converges weakly to
P
almost surely,
but it has the following (almost sure) biased limit:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim Q_{n}}[Y\otimes Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}[X\otimes X]+\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}.

We investigate how the KSD between
P

and
Q_{n}
changes along the sequence.
We examine the kernel choice recommended in
3.4
by setting
q_{m}=0
and
q=2
(referred to as IMQ
+
).
We compare this choice against using
k_{\textup{IMQ}}
alone, which corresponds to the failure case illustrated in
3.1
.

(a)
Off-target sequence with non-converging second moments.

(b)
On-target sequence formed by i.i.d samples from the target.

Figure 1:

Importance of kernel choice for controlling second moments.

(a) For a multivariate Gaussian target
P
, the standard IMQ KSD converges to
0
for an off-target sequence
Q_{n}
that does
not
converge to
P
in
2
-Wasserstein distance.
Meanwhile, a KSD with the proposed IMQ
+
kernel remains bounded away from
0
as required by
3.4
.
(b) For an on-target sequence
P_{n}
drawn i.i.d. from
P
, both KSDs decay to zero, correctly detecting the
2
-Wasserstein convergence.
See
Section

4.1
for more details.

Figure

1a
shows the KSD’s transition along
the approximating sequence for our two kernel choices.
We can see that using the IMQ kernel alone, the KSD decays to zero even though the second moment is not converging to
P
.
Fortunately, as proved in
3.1
, our proposed base kernel does not suffer from this failing.
Rather, its induced KSD enforces second-moment uniform integrability and hence remains bounded away from zero for the off-target sequence
Q_{n}
.

Figure

1b
verifies that both KSDs converge to zero
for an on-target sample sequence
P_{n}
drawn i.i.d. from the target (see Eq.
17
).
This result concords with
3.2
, which guarantees that the KSD converges to zero whenever the approximating sequence converges to
P
in the 2-Wasserstein sense.

4.2
Detecting first moment non-convergence

Next, we turn to a heavy-tailed target distribution.
Past work
(
Gorham and Mackey 2017
;
Barp et al. 2024
)
has shown that the IMQ Langevin KSD can fail to detect weak convergence for this target and hence can also fail to detect convergence in mean.
We exemplify this failure mode using a specific example and show how we can surmount it with a diffusion KSD.

As our target
P,
we use the standard multivariate
t
-distribution
with the degrees of freedom
\nu>1
, which is defined by the density

p(x)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu+d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\nu^{\frac{d}{2}}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}\left(1+\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\nu}\right)^{-\frac{\nu+d}{2}};

we set
d=5
and
\nu=6
in our experiment.
We consider the following approximation sequence:

Q_{n}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n+1}\right)P_{n}+\frac{1}{n+1}\delta_{x_{n}},

where
x_{n}=(n+1)\cdot\mathbf{1}
. As in the previous section, this sequence has a limiting mean with bias
\mathbf{1}
.

Here, we compare the IMQ Lanvegin KSD (LKSD) against a diffusion KSD (DKSD).
As in the previous section, LKSD uses
K=k_{\textup{IMQ}}\mathrm{Id}
as a base kernel.
To define the DKSD, we consider a diffusion matrix
m(x)=(1+\nu^{-1}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2})\mathrm{Id}
.
Recall that the DKSD may be seen as the Langevin KSD (Eq.
2
) defined by the tilted kernel
K(x,y)=m(x)K_{0}(x,y)m(y)^{\top}
, where we use
K_{0}=k_{\textup{IMQ${}^{+}$}}\mathrm{Id}
, the base kernel recommended in
3.4
with
q_{m}=1
and
q=1
(see the last paragraph of
Section

2
).

(a)
Off-target sequence with non-converging means.

(b)
On-target sequence formed by i.i.d samples from the target.

Figure 2:

Importance of kernel choice for controlling first moments.

(a) For a multivariate Student’s
t
target
P
, the standard IMQ Langevin KSD (LKSD) converges to
0
for an off-target sequence
Q_{n}
that does
not
converge to
P
in
1
-Wasserstein distance.
Meanwhile, a diffusion KSD (DKSD) with an appropriate diffusion matrix
m
and an IMQ
+
base kernel remains bounded away from
0
as required by
3.1
.
(b) For an on-target sequence
P_{n}
drawn i.i.d. from
P
, both KSDs decay to zero, correctly detecting the
1
-Wasserstein convergence.
See
Section

4.2
for more details.

Figure
2a
plots the two KSDs along the off-target sequence
Q_{n}
.
We can see that the LKSD decays to zero.
As
3.1
suggests,
since the Student’s
t
-distribution has a decaying score function
\nabla\log p
,
the corresponding Stein RKHS cannot have a growing function with a bounded base kernel, and thus is unable to enforce first-moment uniform integrability.
In contrast, the DKSD sucessfuly detects the mean non-convergence.
Indeed, our choice of the diffusion matrix
m
satisfies our assumptions and enables us to cancel the decay of the score function:
as we show in

K.4
, if
\nu>2,
the
t
-distribution is dissipative (Assumption

3
) and
\alpha=(1-2\nu^{-1});
in fact, it satisfies the uniform dissipativity
condition in
3.4
.
2
2

2

According to Theorem
3.1
,
the allowed choice of the growth
q
of test functions is
q<\nu-4
.
In fact, we may take
q<\nu+\theta-2
with
0<\theta<1,

since
P
has moments up to order
\nu+\theta-2;
see Remark
2
.

This result confirms our theory for the DKSD, as proved in
3.1
.
Finally, as in the previous section,
Figure

2b
shows that both KSDs vanish for an on-target sample sequence
P_{n}
, demonstrating that the KSD detects the
1
-Wasserstein convergence, as
3.2
implies.

4.3
Cautionary tale: mixtures with isolated components

Our final experiment concerns the following distributions:

P=\frac{1}{2}{\cal N}(\mu_{1},\mathrm{Id})+\frac{1}{2}{\cal N}(\mu_{2},\mathrm{Id}),\ Q_{\pi}=\pi{\cal N}(\mu_{1},\mathrm{Id})+(1-\pi){\cal N}(\mu_{2},\mathrm{Id}),

(18)

where
0\leq\pi\leq 1
,
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

{\cal N}(\mu,\mathrm{Id})
denotes a Gaussian distribution with mean
\mu
and diagonal covariance
\mathrm{Id}
.
The two distributions
P
and
Q_{\pi}
share the mixture components, with the difference arising from the variation in the mixing weight
\pi
.
Varying
\pi
shifts the mean of
Q_{\pi}
, and one might hope that the KSD will detect this discrepancy.
The target
P
is indeed supported by our theory,
since Gaussian mixtures are known to be distantly dissipative
(
Gorham et al. 2019
, Example 3)
.
When the distance
\lVert\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\rVert_{2}
between
the two modes is large, however, the KSD is unable to capture the discrepancy
of the mixture ratio
\pi
unless a very large sample size is observed.
Figure
3
illustrates this point, using the kernel of
3.4
, where
q_{m}=0
and
q=1
.
This issue has been noted by
Gorham et al. 2019

and
Wenliang and Kanagawa 2021
.

Figure 3:

Impact of isolated modes on non-convergence detection.

For a two-component multivariate Gaussian mixture
P
with mode separation

\lVert\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\rVert_{2}
, we compare
the IMQ
+
KSD of an on-target sequence
P_{N}
drawn i.i.d. from
P
and an
off-target sequence
Q_{\pi,N}
drawn i.i.d. from a distribution that undersamples mode
1
(with
\pi=1/10
).
The larger the mode separation
\lVert\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\rVert_{2},

the larger the sample size required to distinguish the on- and off-target sequences.
See
Section

4.3
for more details.

The insensitivity can be in part attributed to the RKHS’s lack of adaptivity to the sample.
We first examine this claim using fixed kernels, as in the experiment from
Figure

3
.
To this end, we consider the Langevin KSD with the base kernel of the form of
5
, where
L=L^{(2)}
in Theorem

3.1
,
q_{m}=0
, and
q=1
;
this choice ensures that the Stein RKHS has a function of linear growth.
We study two choices of the translation invariant kernel
\ell
: the IMQ
kernel
k_{\textup{IMQ}}(x,x^{\prime})=\bigl(1+\lVert x-x^{\prime}\rVert_{2}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\bigr)^{-1/2}

and the following Matérn kernel

k_{\mathrm{Mat}}(x,x^{\prime})=\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{3}\lVert x-x^{\prime}\rVert_{2}}{\sigma}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sigma}\lVert x-x^{\prime}\rVert_{2}\right).

As in
3.4
, we term these two kernel choices IMQ
+
and Mat
+
, respectively.
Here, to capture a sensible scale, we set the bandwidth
\sigma
equal to the median (Euclidean) distance calculated from samples drawn from the target distribution
P
.

In this setting, we use an independent sample
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
from
Q_{\pi}
to form a sequence of empirical distributions
Q_{\pi,N}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\leq N}\delta_{X_{i}}

and exactly compute the KSD between
P
and
Q_{\pi,N}.
In
the following, we set
\mu_{1}=-30\cdot\mathbf{1},

\mu_{2}=-10\cdot\mathbf{1}

and
d=5.
With
N
fixed at
500,
we vary the mixture ratio
\pi

from
0
to
1/2
using a regular grid of size
30.
We noticed
that the KSD’s trajectory has different trends depending on the drawn
samples. We therefore repeat this procedure 100 times and provide
a summary.

Figure

4a
presents density estimates of the distribution of KSD values computed from different sample draws, plotted against the mixture ratio.
We observe that for both kernels, their KSDs do not
change along the sequence.

One can hope to improve this sensitivity by adjusting the length scale
\sigma
adaptively.
Here, following the approaches
in statistical hypothesis testing
(
Gretton et al. 2012b
;
Sutherland et al. 2016
;
Jitkrittum et al. 2016
;
Jitkrittum et al. 2017
)
,
we consider choosing a bandwidth by optimizing the
objective
\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q_{\pi,N})^{2}/\sqrt{v_{H_{1}}},
where
v_{H_{1}}=\mathrm{Var}_{X\sim Q_{\pi,N}}\bigl[\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime}\sim Q_{\pi,N}}[k_{p,K}(X,X^{\prime})]\bigr].

This objective is a known proxy for the power of the goodness-of-fit
test based on a KSD test statistic
(
Chwialkowski et al. 2016
;
Liu et al. 2016
)
and can be computed exactly.
The optimization procedure may be interpreted as seeking a bandwidth
that allows us to conclude
P\neq Q_{\pi,N}
using as few sample
points as possible, without using the whole of
Q_{\pi,N}.
We choose
a bandwidth that maximizes the objective from a regular grid between

10^{-3}
and
10^{3}
(in the logarithmic scale with base
10
) with the grid size
20.

(a)
KSDs with fixed length scale base kernels suffer from an insensitivity to changes in the Gaussian mixture proportion
\pi
.

(b)

KSDs with optimized length scale enjoy improved sensitivity to changes in the Gaussian mixture proportion
\pi
.

Figure 4:
Impact of kernel length scale on detecting undersampled mixture components.

To assess the impact of adaptively selecting the length scale of the KSD base kernel, we plot both the distribution of KSD values and their mean value (as a bold dot) over independent samples of
N=500
points drawn i.i.d. from the Gaussian mixture
Q_{\pi}
in
Equation

18
when the target
P=Q_{1/2}
. See
Section

4.3
for more details.

Figure
4b
shows the result. Both kernels successfully manifest
decreasing trends. The IMQ kernel tends to take larger values near

\pi=0,
but stays at the same value past the point
\pi=0.3.
In
contrast, the Matérn kernel keeps the decreasing trend and captures
the discrepancy. The Matérn kernel may therefore be thought of as
more stable and as inducing a more stringent discrepancy measure.

5
Conclusion

In this paper, we presented necessary and sufficient conditions for the diffusion kernel Stein discrepancy to control both weak convergence and convergence of moments.
These theoretical results provide guidance for choosing a reproducing kernel when applying the diffusion kernel Stein discrepancy in practice.
The practical application of our results may be limited by the need to validate the assumptions on the target distribution, like that of dissipativity (
1
).
Developing a discrepancy measure that is adaptive to the target is desirable and left for future work.

AG acknowledges financial support by the Gatsby Charitable Foundation.
The authors thank Chris Oates for his helpful feedback, and Carl-Johann Simon-Gabriel for the constructive discussions.

Appendix A
Proof of
Proposition
3.1
:

Standard KSDs fail to control moments

This appendix presents a proof of
3.1
.
We provide a result that holds for a more general maximum mean discrepancy
[
Gretton et al. 2012a
, MMD,]
.
The claim for the KSD follows once we address the MMD case, since the KSD is an specific instance of the MMD (see
Section

2
).

Recall that for a scalar-valued kernel, the MMD is defined as an IPM (see Eq.
4
for the definition):
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=d_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}(P,Q)
, where

\mathcal{B}_{k}
is the unit ball
\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{k}:\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq 1\}
of the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
If
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset L^{1}(P)\cap L^{1}(Q)
, we have

\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)^{2}=\int\int k(x,y)\differential\bigl(P-Q\bigr)(x)\differential\bigl(P-Q\bigr)(y).

For a finite signed measure
\mu
such that
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset L^{1}(\lvert\mu\rvert)
, we also denote

\lVert\mu\rVert_{k}\coloneqq\sqrt{\int\int k(x,y)\differential\mu(x)\differential\mu(y)}

so that
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=\lVert P-Q\rVert_{k}
.

Here, we generalize our setup to a metric space
(\mathcal{X},d)
, and consider
q
-Wasserstein convergence accordingly:

Definition 6

(
q
-Wasserstein convergence in metric spaces)
.

Let
({\cal X},d)
be a separable metric space. We define the set
of Borel probability measures with finite
q
th moments
by
