{\cal P}_{q}({\cal X})=\left\{\mu:\int d(x,\bar{x})^{q}\differential\mu(x)<\infty\ \text{for some}\ \bar{x}\in{\cal X}\right\},

and denote
{\cal P}_{0}(\mathcal{X})
by
{\cal P}(\mathcal{X})
.
A function
f:{\cal X}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be of
q
-growth
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\lvert f(x)\rvert\leq C(1+d(x,x_{0})^{q})

for some (and then any)
x_{0}\in{\cal X}.
A sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P}({\cal X})

is said to converge to
P
in
q
-Wasserstein convergence
if

\int\lvert f\rvert\differential P_{n}\to\int\lvert f\rvert\differential P

for any continuous function
f
of
q
-growth.

We are ready to provide the result.
The following proposition demonstrates that the MMD cannot detect moment non-convergence when its RKHS lacks
q
-growth functions.

Proposition A.1

(
q
-growth is necessary for enforcing
q
th moment convergence)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d)
be an unbounded separable metric space.
Let
q\in(0,\infty)
and
P\in{\cal P}_{q}(\mathcal{X})
.
Let
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be a kernel satisfying the following growth condition:
there exist some
C,R>0
and some
x_{0}
such that

\sqrt{k(x,x)}\leq C\{1+d(x_{0},x)^{q^{\prime}}\}

for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
with
d(x,x_{0})>R
, where
0<q^{\prime}<q
.
Then, vanishing
\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,\cdot)
does not imply
q
-Wasserstein convergence to
P
.

Before presenting a proof, we briefly remark that

3.1
holds as a corollary of this proposition.
Indeed, under
2
, from the expression of the Stein kernel (Eq.
33
), we have
\sqrt{k_{p,K}(x,x)}\leq C(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})
for some constant
C>0
.
As a result, for
q>1
, the KSD cannot control
q
-Wasserstein convergence.

Proof.

We construct a sequence in
{\cal P}_{q}(\mathcal{X})
that converges to
P
in MMD but not in
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
Let
n\geq 1
be an integer such that there exists a point
x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
with

(n-1)^{1/q}\leq d(x_{0},x_{n})\leq n^{1/q}
.
There are infinitely many such
n
as
\mathcal{X}
is bounded otherwise.
Let
\{n(j)\}_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence of such integers with
x_{j}
the corresponding point.
Then, define a sequence

Q_{j}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\right)P+\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\delta_{x_{j}}.

For
j\geq 1
with
d(x_{j},x_{0})>R
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\lVert P\rVert_{k}+\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\lVert\delta_{x_{j}}\rVert_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\lVert P\rVert_{k}+\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\sqrt{k(x_{j},x_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\lVert P\rVert_{k}+\frac{C}{n(j)+1}\left(1+n(j)^{q^{\prime}/q}\right)\to 0\ (j\to\infty).

Thus,
Q_{j}
converges to
P
in MMD.
On the other hand, for any continuous bounded function
f
,

\displaystyle\int f\differential Q_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\right)\int f\differential P+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n(j)+1}f(x_{j})}_{\to 0}

\displaystyle\to\int f\differential P\ (j\to\infty),

where we have used the boundedness of
f
to diminish the second term in the limit. Hence,
Q_{j}
weakly converges to
P
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\int d(x_{0},\cdot)^{q}\differential Q_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\right)\int d(x_{0},\cdot)^{q}\differential P+\frac{1}{n(j)+1}d(x_{0},x_{j})^{q}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{n(j)+1}\right)\int d(x_{0},\cdot)^{q}\differential P+\frac{n(j)-1}{n(j)+1}.

Taking the limit on both sides implies that
Q_{j}
does not converge to
P
in the
q
th moment.
Thus, we have constructed the desired sequence, and the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix B
Approximating
q
-growth

In this appendix, it is shown that we can construct a Stein RKHS
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
that approximates
q
-growth.
Our strategy is as follows.
First, we construct a vector-valued function
v_{\varepsilon}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v_{\varepsilon}
approximates
q
-growth up to precition
\varepsilon
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon}\}-\varepsilon

for some
{r_{\varepsilon}}>0

—
B.1
serves this purpose.
Note that
v_{\epsilon}
may not belong to the RKHS.
Thus, we approximate it using a function of the RKHS; we provide two such results in
Section

B.2
.

B.1
Preparatory results

Lemma B.1

(Existence of
q
-growth approximation)
.

For
r,\delta>0,
define a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-function

1_{r,\delta}(x)=h_{r,\delta}(\lVert x\rVert_{2})
with

h_{r,\delta}(t)=\frac{f(r+\delta-t)}{f(r+\delta-t)+f(t-r)}

and
f(t)=t^{3}1_{[0,\infty)}(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
q\in[0,\infty).

Assume the dissipativity condition in Assumption
1
with

\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{0}.
Suppose Assumption
2

holds for some constants
c_{m}>0
and
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
;
if
q_{m}=1,
additionally assume
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2).

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there exist positive constants
r,\delta,\eta

such that

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\varepsilon 1\{r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta\}

where
\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}=1-1_{r,\delta},

v(x)=-w_{q-2}(x)x

with
w_{q-2}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q-2)/2}

and the constant
\eta

is determined by the following quantities:
\alpha,

\beta_{1},

\beta_{0},

\lambda_{m},

\tau,

q_{m},
and
q.

In particular, we may take
r+\delta\geq 1
.

Proof.

Given positive constants
r,\delta,
our proof
separately considers the three regimes:
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r,

r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta,

and
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta.
We then choose suitable
r,\delta.

Below we make use of the following relation that holds for differentiable functions
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(\gamma g\bigr)(x)=\gamma(x){\cal T}_{P}^{m}g(x)+\langle m(x),\nabla\gamma(x)\otimes g(x)\rangle.

(19)

First, if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r,
we have
\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}(x)=0

and thus
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)=0,
for any
choice of
r
and any
v
in the domain of
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}.

In the following, we consider
v(x)=-w_{q-2}(x)x
as claimed.

In the regime
r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta,
we show that for any
\varepsilon>0,

we can choose
r,\delta>0
such that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)\geq-\varepsilon.

With
\lVert x\rVert_{2}=t
and
\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)=1-h_{r,\delta}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)

\displaystyle=\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}(x){\cal T}_{P}^{m}v(x)-h_{r,\delta}^{{}^{\prime}}\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\langle m(x),x\otimes v(x)\rangle\frac{1}{\lVert x\rVert_{2}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}(t)\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)\left\{-\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}x-\frac{2(q/2-1)t^{2}}{\tau^{2}+t^{2}}\frac{\left\langle m(x),x\otimes x\right\rangle}{t^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\hphantom{=\tilde{w}(t)\bigl(1-h_{r,\delta}(t)\bigr)}\quad\left.-\frac{h^{\prime}_{r,\delta}(t)}{\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)}\frac{\left\langle m(x),x\otimes x\right\rangle}{t^{2}}t\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\tilde{w}(t)\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)\left[\alpha t^{2}-\beta_{1}t-\beta_{0}-\lambda_{m}\left(q-2+\frac{h^{\prime}_{r,\delta}(t)}{\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)}t\right)(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \tilde{w}(t)\left[\tilde{h}_{r,\delta}(t)\cdot\left\{\alpha t^{2}-\beta_{1}t-\beta_{0}-\lambda_{m}\left(q-2+\omega_{r,\delta}(t)\right)(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})\right\}\right.\\
\hphantom{=\tilde{w}(t)}\quad\left.-\lambda_{m}r\cdot h^{\prime}_{r,\delta}(t)(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})\right]\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{aligned} \tilde{w}(t)\left[\frac{(t-r)^{3}}{(r+\delta-t)^{3}+(t-r)^{3}}\cdot\left\{\alpha t^{2}-\beta_{1}t-\beta_{0}-\lambda_{m}(q+2)(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})\right\}\right.\\
\hphantom{=\tilde{w}(t)}\quad\left.-\lambda_{m}r\frac{3\delta(r+\delta-t)^{2}(t-r)^{2}}{\{(r+\delta-t)^{3}+(t-r)^{3}\}^{2}}(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})\right]\end{aligned}

(20)

where
\tilde{w}(t)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+t^{2}\bigr)^{q/2-1}

and
\omega_{r,\delta}(t)\coloneqq 3\delta(r+\delta-t)^{2}/\{(r+\delta-t)^{3}+(t-r)^{3}\}
;
the first inequality is derived from the dissipativity
(
1
) and the growth condition on

\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
(
2
); to obtain the second inequality, we
have used
\omega_{r,\delta}(t)\leq 4
for
r<t<r+\delta
.
In the lower bound (
20
), we have a quadratic function inside the braces in the first term,
where, by assumption, the quadratic term has positive coefficients

\alpha
and
\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)
for
q_{m}=0

and
q_{m}=1,
respectively. Thus, by choosing
r>0
(independent
of
\delta)
appropriately, we can make this quadratic function both
positive and increasing in the interval
(r,r+\delta).
We denote
this value of
r
by
r_{0}.

The lower bound (
20
) may become negative,
but we show that its magnitude can be made arbitrarily small by choosing

r
and
\delta
judiciously. To see this, note that the second
term of (
20
) dominates the first iff

\frac{\alpha}{3\lambda_{m}}\frac{\theta(1+\theta^{3})}{(1+\theta)^{2}}\frac{\delta}{r}\cdot\frac{t^{2}-\lambda_{m}(q+2)/\alpha\cdot t^{q_{m}+1}-(\beta_{1}/\alpha)t-\beta_{0}/\alpha-\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)/\alpha}{t^{q_{m}+1}+c_{a}}\leq 1,

(21)

where
\theta=(t-r)/(r+\delta-t)\in(0,\infty).
By taking sufficiently
large
r,
we may assume that for any
t>r,

\frac{t^{q_{m}+1}+c_{m}}{t^{2}-\lambda_{m}(q+2)/\alpha\cdot t^{q_{m}+1}-(\beta_{1}/\alpha)t-\beta_{0}/\alpha-\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)/\alpha}\leq C

for some constant
C>0.
If
q_{m}=0,
we may choose any

C>0,
whereas if
q_{m}=1,
we need
C>\{1-\lambda_{m}(q+2)/\alpha\}^{-1}.

Indeed, such
r
can be chosen by examining the following conditions:

\begin{cases}0\leq Ct^{2}-\bigl[C\bigl\{\frac{\beta_{1}}{\alpha}+\lambda_{m}\frac{q+2}{\alpha}\bigr\}+1\bigr]t-C\left\{\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha}+\frac{\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)}{\alpha}+\frac{c_{m}}{C}\right\}&\text{if }q_{m}=0,\\
0\leq\bigl[C\{1-\frac{\lambda_{m}(q+2)}{\alpha}\}-1\bigr]t^{2}-C\frac{\beta_{1}}{\alpha}t-C\left\{\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha}+\frac{\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)}{\alpha}+\frac{c_{m}}{C}\right\}&\text{if }q_{m}=1.\end{cases}

Let
r_{1}
be a value of such
r.
Using
r=r_{0}\lor r_{1},

the inequality (
21
) implies

\frac{\theta(1+\theta^{3})}{(1+\theta)^{2}}\leq\frac{3\lambda_{m}C}{\alpha}\frac{r}{\delta}.

(22)

Then, the magnitude of the second term is evaluated as follows: we
have

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(t)=\left\{\tau^{2}+\left(\frac{\theta(1+\theta^{3})}{(1+\theta)^{2}}\frac{1+\theta}{1+\theta^{3}}\delta+r\right)^{2}\right\}^{q/2-1}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\tau^{q-2}}&q\leq 2\\
\left\{\tau^{2}+\left(\frac{4\lambda_{m}C}{\alpha}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\right\}^{q/2-1}&q>2\end{cases},

and

\displaystyle 3\lambda_{m}r\delta\frac{(r+\delta-t)^{2}(t-r)^{2}}{\{(r+\delta-t)^{3}+(t-r)^{3}\}^{2}}(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1})

\displaystyle=3\lambda_{m}\frac{\theta^{2}}{(1+\theta)^{4}}\frac{(1+\theta)^{6}}{(1+\theta^{3}){}^{2}}\left\{c_{m}+\left(\frac{\theta}{1+\theta}\delta+r\right)^{q_{m}+1}\right\}\frac{r}{\delta}

\displaystyle=3\lambda_{m}\left(\frac{\theta(1+\theta^{3})}{(1+\theta)^{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{(1+\theta)^{6}}{(1+\theta^{3}){}^{4}}\left\{c_{m}+\left(\frac{1+\theta}{1+\theta^{3}}\frac{\theta(1+\theta^{3})}{(1+\theta)^{2}}\delta+r\right)^{q_{m}+1}\right\}\frac{r}{\delta}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\eqref{eq:norm-indicator-theta-eval}}}{\leq}\frac{27\lambda_{m}^{3}C^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\frac{123+55\sqrt{5}}{32}\right)\left\{c_{m}+\left(\frac{4\lambda_{m}C}{\alpha}+1\right)^{q_{m}+1}r^{q_{m}+1}\right\}\cdot\left(\frac{r}{\delta}\right)^{3}.

(23)

The product of these provides the desired estimate of the magnitude.
For a given
r,
as
\delta
grows, the evaluation (
23
)
decays at the rate of
\delta^{-3}.
Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0,

we can choose sufficiently large
\delta
such that the domination
by the second term in (
20
) is at most
by
\varepsilon;
i.e.,
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v\bigr)(x)\geq-\varepsilon

for
r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta.
We denote such choice of
\delta

by
\delta_{\varepsilon}(r).

Finally, consider the regime
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta,
where
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)=\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x).

We show that a suitable value of
r+\delta
yields
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}

for some
\eta>0.
By Assumption
1
and the
growth condition on
\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)

\displaystyle\geq\tilde{w}(\lVert x\rVert_{2})\left(\alpha\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}-\beta_{1}\lVert x\rVert_{2}-\beta_{0}-\lambda_{m}(q-2)\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}{(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2})}\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{m}+1}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\tilde{f}\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\left(1+\frac{\tau^{2}}{\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}-1}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q},

where

\tilde{f}(t)=\left\{\alpha-\frac{1}{t^{2}}\left(\beta_{1}t+\beta_{0}+\lambda_{m}(q-2)\bigl(c_{m}+t^{q_{m}+1}\bigr)1\{q>2\}\right)\right\}.

Due to the monotonicity of
\tilde{f},
we have
\delta_{0}
such
that
\tilde{f}(\delta_{0})=0.
By the requirement on
r_{0},
it
turns out
r_{0}>\delta_{0}.
Therefore, if
q\geq 2,
we
have
{\cal T}_{P}g(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}
for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r_{0}

and
\eta=\tilde{f}(r_{0});
if
q<2,
we have
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}g(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert^{q}

for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r_{0}\lor\tau^{2}\bigl(2^{2/\lvert q-2\rvert}-1\bigr)^{-1}

and
\eta=2^{-1}\text{$\tilde{f}(r_{0})$}.

The consideration for the above three regimes of
\lVert x\rVert_{2}
suggests
that we should choose
\delta=\delta_{\varepsilon}(r),
and
r=r_{0}\lor r_{1}

if
q\geq 2
or else
r=r_{0}\lor r_{1}\lor\tau^{2}\bigl(2^{2/\lvert q-2\rvert}-1\bigr)^{-1}
. This choice yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\varepsilon 1\{r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta\}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

∎

Corollary B.1

(Concrete choices of
r
and
\delta
in
B.1
)
.

Lemma
B.1

holds for

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}r_{1}&q\geq 2\\
r_{1}\lor\tau^{2}\bigl(2^{2/\lvert q-2\rvert}-1\bigr)^{-1}&0\leq q<2\end{cases}

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=W_{r}r\cdot\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}},

where

r_{1}=\begin{cases}\frac{\{\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}+1+\sqrt{[\{\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}+1]^{2}+4\alpha\left\{\beta_{0}+\lambda_{m}c_{a}(q+2)+c_{m}\right\}}}{2\alpha}&q_{m}=0\\
\frac{2\beta_{1}+2\sqrt{\beta_{1}^{2}+2\tilde{\alpha}\{\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)+\beta_{0}\}+\tilde{\alpha}c_{m}}}{2\tilde{\alpha}}&q_{m}=1,\end{cases}

\displaystyle W_{r}^{3}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bigl(\tau^{-(q-2)}\lor\bar{w}_{0}(r)\bigr)27\lambda_{m}^{3}\left(\frac{123+55\sqrt{5}}{32}\right)\left\{c_{m}+\left(4\lambda_{m}+1\right)r\right\}&q_{m}=0\\
\bigl(\tau^{-(q-2)}\lor\bar{w}_{1}(r)\bigr)\frac{4\cdot 27\lambda_{m}}{\{\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}^{2}}\left(\frac{123+55\sqrt{5}}{32}\right)\left\{c_{m}+\left(\frac{8\lambda_{m}}{\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\right\}&q_{m}=1,\end{cases}

\bar{w}_{0}(r)=\{\tau^{2}+\left(4\lambda_{m}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\}^{q/2-1},

and
\bar{w}_{1}(r)=\bigl[\tau^{2}+\bigl\{2\lambda_{m}/\bigl(\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)\bigr)+1^{2}\bigr\}r^{2}\bigr]^{q/2-1}.

Thus,
r+\delta=(1+W_{r}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}})r.
In particular,
we may take
r\geq 1
to ensure
r+\delta>1.

Proof.

We follow the notation in the proof of
B.1
.
We consider the two cases
q_{m}=0
and
q_{m}=1

separately.

We first examine the case
q_{m}=0.
Here, by checking the
requirement on
r_{0},
we obtain

\displaystyle r_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)+\sqrt{\left(\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)\right)^{2}+4\alpha\left(\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)+\beta_{0}\right)}}{2\alpha}

and with
C=\alpha
in the proof of
B.1
,
the condition on
r_{1}
implies

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\{\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}+1+\sqrt{[\{\beta_{1}+\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}+1]^{2}+4\alpha\left\{\beta_{0}+\lambda_{m}c_{a}(q+2)+c_{m}\right\}}}{2\alpha}>r_{0}.

We then obtain
\delta_{\varepsilon}(r)=W_{r}r\varepsilon^{-1/3}

with

W_{r}^{3}=\bar{w}(r)\cdot 27\lambda_{m}^{3}\left(\frac{123+55\sqrt{5}}{32}\right)\left\{c_{m}+\left(4\lambda_{m}+1\right)r\right\}

and
\bar{w}(r)=\left\{\tau^{2}+\left(4\lambda_{m}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\right\}^{q/2-1}\lor\tau^{-(q-2)}.

Next, we similarly investigate the case
q_{m}=1.
With

\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2),
we have

r_{0}=\frac{\beta_{1}+\sqrt{\beta_{1}^{2}+4\tilde{\alpha}\{\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)+\beta_{0}\}}}{2\tilde{\alpha}}

and by choosing
C=2\alpha(\tilde{\alpha})^{-1},
we obtain

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\beta_{1}+2\sqrt{\beta_{1}^{2}+2\tilde{\alpha}\{\lambda_{m}c_{m}(q+2)+\beta_{0}\}+\tilde{\alpha}c_{m}}}{2\tilde{\alpha}}

\displaystyle>r_{0}

Thus,
\delta_{\varepsilon}=W_{r}r\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}
with

W_{r}^{3}=\bar{w}(r)\frac{4\cdot 27\lambda_{m}^{3}}{\{\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)\}^{2}}\left(\frac{123+55\sqrt{5}}{32}\right)\left\{c_{m}+\left(\frac{8\lambda_{m}}{\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\right\}

and

\bar{w}(r)=\left\{\tau^{2}+\left(\frac{8\lambda_{m}}{\alpha-\lambda_{m}(q+2)}+1\right)^{2}r^{2}\right\}^{q/2-1}\lor\tau^{-(q-2)}.

∎

B.2
Proof of
3.2
:
Lemma 3.2

(Kernel choice for
q
-growth approximation)
.

Lemma
B.1
provides a concrete function to approximate
q
-growth.
To prove Lemma
3.2
, we
approximate this function using an RKHS function.
Our proof is divided into two parts:
the first proof deals with the general universal kernel, while the
second proof deals with the translation-invariant kernel case (see
Corollary
B.2
).

B.2.1
Proof via universality

Lemma B.2

(Stein RKHS with universal kernel approximates
q
-growth)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty).
Let
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

be a matrix-valued kernel defined by

K(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)\left(L(x,y)+\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)\mathrm{Id}\right)w_{q-1}(y),

where
w_{q-1}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q-1)/2}

with
\tau>0;

L
is universal
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{d}\bigr);

and

\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)=\frac{\tau^{2}+\langle x,y\rangle}{\sqrt{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{\tau^{2}+\lVert y\rVert_{2}^{2}}}.

Then, under the same assumptions as in
B.1
,

{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
approximates
q
-growth.

Proof.

B.1
provides a concrete function approximating
q
-growth:
for any
\varepsilon>0,
, there
exist positive constants
r,\delta,\eta
such that

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(v-1_{r,\delta}v\bigr)(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}1\{r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta\}

(24)

where

v(x)=-w_{q-2}(x)x
,

r+\delta\geq 1,
and
1_{r,\delta}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
is a function
defined in
B.1
satisfying

1_{r,\delta}(x)=1
if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta,

1_{r,\delta}(x)=0

if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta,
and
0<1_{r,\delta}(x)<1,
otherwise.
The proof would be complete if
v-1_{r,\delta}v
were an element of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
Our choice of kernel indeed implies
v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
, as we detail in the next paragraph;
however, the function
1_{r,\delta}v
may not be an element of
\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
Thus, we approximate the latter using a function in the RKHS defined by kernel
L_{w_{q-1}}(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)L(x,y)w_{q-1}(y)
.

Let us first examine the claim
v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
First recall that for two kernels
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
, the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{K_{1}+K_{2}}
of the sum kernel
K_{1}+K_{2}
is given by functions
\{h_{1}+h_{2}:h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{K_{1}},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{K_{2}}\}

[see, e.g.,
Carmeli et al. 2010
, Proposition 3.1]
;
in particular, we have
\mathcal{H}_{K_{1}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{K_{1}+K_{2}}
(the same goes for
\mathcal{H}_{K_{2}})
.
Note that the RKHS of the kernel
\bar{k}_{w,\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)\coloneqq w_{q-1}(x)w_{q-1}(y)\bar{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)
contains tilted linear functions of the form
w_{q-2}(x)\langle a,x\rangle
with
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since it is the sum of two scalar kernels, one of which is a linear kernel tilted by
w_{q-2}
.
Each component of
v
therefore belongs to
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{k}_{w,\mathrm{lin}}}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{k}_{w,\mathrm{lin}}\mathrm{Id}}
consists of vector-valued functions with each component in
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{k}_{w,\mathrm{lin}}}
, we have
v\in\mathcal{H}_{\bar{k}_{w,\mathrm{lin}}\mathrm{Id}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{K}
.

We choose our approximation to
1_{r,\delta}v
as follows. Define
\tilde{v}(x)=
-1_{r,\delta}(x)\cdot w_{-1}(x)x
.
so that
1_{r,\delta}v(x)=w_{q-1}(x)\tilde{v}(x).
Since
\nabla 1_{r,\delta}(x)

is supported in
\{\lVert x\rVert\leq r+\delta\}
,
\tilde{v}
is an element
of
{\cal C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).
By the
{\cal C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universality
of the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{L},
for
\rho>0
(specified below), we have an
element
v_{\rho}=(v_{\rho}^{1},\dots,v_{\rho}^{d})\in\mathcal{H}_{L}

approximating
\tilde{v}
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\lvert v_{\rho}^{i}(x)-\tilde{v}^{i}(x)\rvert\lor\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\lvert\partial_{x^{j}}v_{\rho}^{i}(x)-\partial_{x^{j}}\tilde{v}^{i}(x)\rvert\right)\leq\rho.

This in turn implies

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert v_{\rho}(x)-\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\rho\ \text{and\ }\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\nabla v_{\rho}(x)-\nabla\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq d\rho.

(25)

Note that we have, with the shorthand
w=w_{q-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(v-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(v-w\tilde{v}\bigr)(x)+\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w\tilde{v}-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}1\{r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\geq}\quad-\lvert\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w\tilde{v}-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)\rvert,

and
wv_{\rho}\in\mathcal{H}_{L_{w_{q}}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{K}
.
Thus, our goal is to choose
\rho
such that the error
\lvert\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w\tilde{v}-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)\rvert

grows more slowly than
\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}
if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta

and can be made small if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta
.

We evaluate the error of
wv_{\rho}
when applied to the Stein
operator
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}
(see also Eq.
19
):

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}wv_{\rho}(x)-\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}w\tilde{v}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\begin{aligned} &w(x)\left(2\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}\lVert v_{\rho}(x)-\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{2}+\lvert q-1\rvert\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}}{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert^{2}}\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lVert v_{\rho}(x)-\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{2}\right.\\
&\hphantom{w(x)}\left.\quad+\text{$\sqrt{d}$}\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lVert\nabla v_{\rho}(x)-\nabla\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\right)\end{aligned}

(26)

We bound the estimate (
26
)
to prove the statement for
q_{m}=0.

Using the universality properties (
25
),
we can evaluate (
26
) as

\displaystyle\lvert{\cal T}_{P}wv_{\rho}(x)-{\cal T}_{P}w\tilde{v}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d}\rho\cdot w(x)\left(2\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}+\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}}{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert^{2}}\lvert q-1\rvert\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+d\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\rho\cdot w(x)\pi(\lVert x\rVert_{2}),

where we have defined
\pi
by

\displaystyle\pi(\lVert x\rVert_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sqrt{d}\left\{2\lambda_{b}\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)+\lambda_{m}\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}}{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert^{2}}(q-1)_{+}\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)+\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d}\left\{2\lambda_{b}\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)+\lambda_{m}(q-1)_{+}\bigl(c_{m}\tau^{-2}+1\bigr)+\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\right\}

The function
\pi
satisfies
w(x)\pi\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)<C\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}

with

C=\bigl(1+\tau^{2}\bigr)^{q/2}\left[2\lambda_{b}+\lambda_{m}d+\left\{2\lambda_{b}+\lambda_{m}(q-1)_{+}\left(c_{m}\tau^{-2}+1\right)+\lambda_{m}c_{m}d\right\}\right]

for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta\geq 1.
Consequently,

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}w\tilde{v}(x)-\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}wv_{\rho}(x)>-\rho C\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta\}-\rho\max_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta}w(x)\pi\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr).

(27)

With the evaluation (
27
),
we conclude the proof for the linear case
q_{m}=0.
We
choose
\rho>0
such that

\rho\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta C^{-1}\land\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}\left(\max_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta}w(x)\pi\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\right)^{-1}

to ensure
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}w\tilde{v}(x)-\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}wv_{\rho}(x)\geq 1/2\cdot\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta\}-\eta\varepsilon/4.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(v-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(v-w\tilde{v}\bigr)(x)+\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w\tilde{v}-wv_{\rho}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}1\{r<\lVert x\rVert_{2}<r+\delta\}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\geq}\ -\frac{\eta}{2}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta\}-\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\eta}{2}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{2}.

The conclusion follows for

v_{\varepsilon}=2\eta^{-1}(v-w_{q-1}v_{\rho})

and
r_{\varepsilon}=r+\delta.

∎

B.2.2
Convolution construction of
q
-growth function

In this section, we provide an alternative, constructive proof of RKHS
q
-growth approximation.

Lemma B.3

(Constructive approximation to the function from
B.1
)
.

Let
q\geq 0,
and

w_{q-2}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q-2)/2}

where
\tau>0.

Let
v(x)=-w_{q-2}(x)x
.
For
\varepsilon>0,
choose
r,\delta,\eta
such that

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}v)(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\}-\varepsilon,

where
\tilde{1}_{r,\delta}=1-1_{r,\delta}(x)
and
1_{r,\delta}(x)

is defined as in Lemma
B.1
. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)

be a probability density function. For
\rho>0
, define

T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}(x)=\rho^{-d}\int\tilde{v}(x-y)\varphi(y/\rho)\differential y,

where
\tilde{v}(x)=-1_{r,\delta}(x)w_{-\theta}(x)\cdot x
with
\theta\in[0,1]
.
Assume the dissipativity condition in Assumption
1

with
\alpha>0
. Assume that the growth conditions
in Assumption
2
with
\lambda_{m}>0
and
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
q_{m}=1,
assume the following two conditions: (a)
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+2),

and (b) there exists positive constants
r_{\varphi},

C_{\varphi},

\gamma\geq d-1
such that

\lvert\varphi(x)\rvert\leq C_{\varphi}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)}

for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r_{\varphi}.
Suppose
\mu_{1}\coloneqq\int\lVert x\rVert_{2}\varphi(x)\differential x<\infty.

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0,
we may choose
\rho
such that the
function
\mathring{v}_{\varepsilon}=2\eta^{-1}\bigl(v-w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}\bigr)

satisfies

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\mathring{v}_{\varepsilon}(x)\geq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\bigl\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon}\bigr\}-\varepsilon

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some
r_{\varepsilon}>0.
In particular,

\rho
and
r_{\varepsilon}
are given as follows:

\rho=\begin{cases}\frac{\eta}{4}(1\land\delta^{2})\Bigl(2\land\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}^{-\{q\lor(1-\theta)\}}\Bigr)U_{3}^{-1}&\text{if}\ q_{m}=0,\\
\frac{\eta}{4}(1\land\delta^{2})\left(2\tilde{C}_{\theta}\land U_{3,\theta}^{-1}\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}^{-(q+1)}\right)\land 1,&\text{if}\ q_{m}=1,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle r_{\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}r+\delta&q_{m}=0,\\
2\bigl\{(r+\delta)\lor r_{\varphi}\bigr\}\lor r_{\eta},&q_{m}=1,\end{cases}

respectively, where

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\theta}=\left\{(\tau^{2}+1)^{q/2}\left(4\lambda_{b}+\lambda_{m}\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert(c_{m}+1)\right)U_{1,\theta}\right\}^{-1},

U_{1,\theta}=\mu_{1}\{(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}+3\}
,

U_{2,\theta}=\mu_{1}\left\{(5\theta+\theta^{2})\tau^{-\theta-1}+6(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}+72+\frac{35+13\sqrt{13}}{12}+6+2^{3+2/3}\right\}

and

U_{3,\theta}=\bigl(\tau^{2}+1\bigr)^{\lvert q+\theta-1\rvert/2}\left\{2\lambda_{b}U_{1,\theta}\bigl(\tau^{-1}+1\bigr)+\lambda_{m}\left(\frac{\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert}{2\tau}U_{1,\theta}+U_{2,\theta}d\right)\bigl(c_{m}\tau^{-1}+1\bigr)\right\}.

and

r_{\eta}=\left\{2^{2+\gamma}\cdot 3(1+(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta})\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\cdot\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)(1\lor\delta^{-1})\eta^{-1}\cdot C_{\varphi}\frac{\pi^{d/2}(r+\delta)^{d}}{\Gamma(d/2+1)}\right\}^{1/\gamma}.

Proof.

The proof essentially proceeds as in the proof of Lemma
3.2

in
Section

B.2
,
and we use the same notation.
Our objective is to construct an approximation to
x\mapsto-1_{r,\delta}(x)w_{q-2}(x)x
.
Let
v_{0}(x)=-w_{-\theta}(x)\cdot x
.
The proof here differs in that we approximate
\tilde{v}=1_{r,\delta}v_{0}

with

T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}(x)=\frac{1}{\rho^{d}}\int\tilde{v}(x-y)\varphi(y/\rho)\differential y,

where
\rho>0
.
We then show that
w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}
is the desired approximation,
replacing its counterpart in the proof in Section
B.2
;
we obtain
\rho
and
r_{\varepsilon}
explicitly.

We first clarify approximation properties of
T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}.

Note that
v_{0}
satisfies

\lVert v_{0}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{1-\theta},\lVert\nabla v_{0}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta},\ \text{and}\ \lVert\nabla^{2}v_{0}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(5\theta+\theta^{2})\tau^{-\theta-1}.

In particular,
\nabla\tilde{v}
is continuous and uniformly bounded,
and hence we have
\nabla T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}=T_{\varphi,\rho}\nabla\tilde{v}

by the mean value theorem and
[
Dudley 1999
, Corollary A.5]
.
Applying Lemmas
I.1
,
I.2
,

K.1
with
g=v_{0}
and
w\equiv 1
(according
to the notation therein) implies that there exist some constants
U_{1,\theta}
,

U_{2,\theta}
such that

\displaystyle\lVert T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}(x)-\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\rho\bigl(1\lor\delta^{-1}\bigr)U_{1,\theta}(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}(x)-\nabla\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\rho\bigl(1\lor\delta^{-2}\bigr)U_{2,\theta}(r+\delta)^{1-\theta},

where

\displaystyle U_{1,\theta}=\mu_{1}\{(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}+3\}

\displaystyle U_{2,\theta}=\mu_{1}\left\{(5\theta+\theta^{2})\tau^{-\theta-1}+6(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}+72+\frac{35+13\sqrt{13}}{12}+6+2^{3+2/3}\right\}

and
\mu_{1}=\int\lVert x\rVert_{2}\varphi(x)\differential x
(here, we have used
r\geq 1
and thus
r+\delta\geq 1
, see
B.1
).

In both cases
q_{m}=0
and
q_{m}=1,
we need
to choose
\rho
such that we can bound the error of
T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}

when applied to the Stein operator by
\eta\varepsilon/4,
within
the ball of radius
r_{\varepsilon}.
The error is evaluated as

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w_{q+\theta-2}\tilde{v}\bigr)(x)-\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}\bigr)(x)\right|\leq\rho\bigl(1\lor\delta^{-2}\bigr)\cdot e\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr),

where

\displaystyle e\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q+\theta-1)/2}\left\{2\lambda_{b}U_{1,\theta}(\tau^{-1}+1)\right.

\displaystyle\hphantom{(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q+\theta-1)/2}}\left.\qquad+\lambda_{m}\left(\frac{\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert}{2\tau}U_{1,\theta}+U_{2,\theta}d\right)(c_{m}\tau^{-1}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{m}})\right\}.

Furthermore, we have
e\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)\leq U_{3,\theta}(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}(1\lor\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q+\theta-1+q_{m}})

with

U_{3,\theta}=\bigl(\tau^{2}+1\bigr)^{\lvert q+\theta-1\rvert/2}\left\{2\lambda_{b}U_{1}\bigl(\tau^{-1}+1\bigr)+\lambda_{m}\left(\frac{\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert}{2\tau}U_{1,\theta}+U_{2,\theta}d\right)\bigl(c_{m}\tau^{-1}+1\bigr)\right\}.

If
r_{\varepsilon}\geq r+\delta\geq 1
, we have

\max_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r_{\varepsilon}}e(\lVert x\rVert_{2})\leq U_{3,\theta}r_{\varepsilon}^{(q+q_{m})\lor(1-\theta)}.

Therefore, for
\varepsilon>0
, we require

\rho\leq\frac{\eta\varepsilon}{4}U_{3,\theta}^{-1}(1\land\delta^{2})r_{\varepsilon}^{-\{(q+q_{m})\lor(1-\theta)\}}.

An additional condition is imposed on
\rho
to make sure that the absolute
error of
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}\bigl(w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}\bigr)(x)

is bounded by
\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}/2
for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon}\geq 1.

We first address the linear case
q_{m}=0
.
Since
e\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)<U_{3,\theta}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}

for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\varepsilon},

we require
\rho\leq\eta U_{3,\theta}^{-1}(1\land\delta^{2})/2.
With
\rho=\eta U_{3}^{-1}(1\land\delta^{2})(2\land\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}^{-\{q\lor(1-\theta)\}})/4,

the conclusion holds for
\mathring{v}_{\varepsilon}=2\eta^{-1}\bigl(v-w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}\bigr)

and
r_{\varepsilon}=(r+\delta).

We next deal with the quadratic case
q_{m}=1.
We first
require
\rho\leq 1.
Then, by the assumption on
\varphi,
for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r_{\varphi},

we have

\displaystyle\rho^{-d}\varphi(x/\rho)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varphi}\rho^{\gamma+1-d}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varphi}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)}.

By Lemma
I.3
, we have for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq 2\{(r+\delta)\lor r_{\varphi}\},

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{(1+\gamma)}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\nabla\tilde{v}(y)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}C_{\varphi}\lambda(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta\})\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{2^{(1+\gamma)}3(1+(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta})C_{\varphi}\lambda(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r+\delta\})}_{C_{\varphi,1}}(1\lor\delta^{-1})(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)},

where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure.
For
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r+\delta\geq 1,
letting
v_{\rho}\coloneqq T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}
and using
Equation

19
give

\displaystyle\left|{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(w_{q+\theta-2}\tilde{v})(x)-{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(w_{q+\theta-2}v_{\rho})(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq w_{q+\theta-2}(x)\left(2\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}\lVert\tilde{v}(x)-v_{\rho}(x)\rVert_{2}+\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert\frac{\lVert x\rVert_{2}}{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}\lVert\tilde{v}(x)-v_{\rho}(x)\rVert_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}\quad+w_{q+\theta-2}(x)d\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lVert\nabla v_{\rho}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq w_{q+\theta-2}(x)\left(2\lambda_{b}(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})+\frac{\lambda_{m}\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert\lVert x\rVert_{2}}{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)\right)U_{1,\theta}\rho(1\lor\delta^{-1})(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}\quad+w_{q+\theta-2}(x)\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)C_{\varphi,1}(1\lor\delta^{-1})(r+\delta)^{1-\theta}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(1+\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\left\{4\lambda_{b}+\lambda_{m}\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert\left(c_{m}+1\right)\right\}U_{1,\theta}\rho(1\lor\delta^{-1})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}\quad+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\cdot\lambda_{m}d\left(c_{m}+1\right)C_{\varphi,1}(1\lor\delta^{-1})\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-\gamma}.

Then, we choose
r_{\eta}\geq r_{\varphi}\lor 1
such that

\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\cdot\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)C_{\varphi,1}(1\lor\delta^{-1})r_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq\frac{\eta}{2};

specifically, we use

\displaystyle r_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\left\{2\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\cdot\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)C_{\varphi,1}(1\lor\delta^{-1})\eta^{-1}\right\}^{1/\gamma}

(28)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{2^{2+\gamma}}{\eta}\cdot 3\left\{1+\frac{(1+\theta)}{\tau^{\theta}}\right\}\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\cdot\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)(1\lor\delta^{-1})\cdot C_{\varphi}\frac{\pi^{d/2}(r+\delta)^{d}}{\Gamma(d/2+1)}\right]^{1/\gamma}.

Following the proof of Lemma
3.2
,
we may choose
r_{\varepsilon}=2\{(r+\delta)\lor r_{\varphi}\}\lor r_{\eta}.

With this choice, we require
\rho\leq\eta\tilde{C}(1\land\delta)/2,

where

\tilde{C}=\left\{(\tau^{2}+1)^{q/2}\left(4\lambda_{b}+\lambda_{m}\lvert q+\theta-2\rvert(c_{m}+1)\right)U_{1,\theta}\right\}^{-1}.

Thus, we set
\rho
as

\rho=\frac{\eta}{4}(1\land\delta^{2})\left(2\tilde{C}\land U_{3}^{-1}\varepsilon r_{\varepsilon}^{-(q+1)}\right)\land 1,

where
r_{\varepsilon}=2\{(r+\delta)\lor r_{\varphi}\}\lor r_{\eta}.

With this value of
\rho,
the conclusion holds for
\mathring{v}_{\varepsilon}=2\eta^{-1}\bigl(v-w_{q+\theta-2}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}\bigr)

and
r_{\varepsilon}=2\{(r+\delta)\lor r_{\varphi}\}\lor r_{\eta}.

∎

Lemma B.4

(Concrete expressions of constants in
B.3
)
.

Let
q\geq 0.
Specify the convolution
function
\varphi
in Lemma
B.3
by
\varphi_{q}

in Lemma
G.7
. Then, the quadratic case
of Lemma
B.3
holds with

r_{\varepsilon}=\left\{2\lor c_{P,d,q,\tau,\theta}(1\lor\delta^{-1/d})\right\}(r+\delta),

where
c_{P,d,q,\tau,\theta}
is a constant given by

\left[4\bigl(1+\tau^{2}\bigr)^{q/2}\lambda_{m}3\{1+(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}\}\frac{\pi{}^{d-2}\Gamma(q/2+1)^{2}2^{4d+3q+6}(c_{m}+1)\eta^{-1}}{\mathrm{B}(d/2,q+1)}\right]^{1/d}.

Proof.

By
[
Wendland 2004
, Proposition 5.6 (3)]
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{q}(x)^{2}

\displaystyle=2^{q}(2\pi)^{d}\Gamma(q/2+1)^{2}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(d+q)}J_{(d+q)/2}\bigl(\lVert x\rVert_{2}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{q}(2\pi)^{d}\Gamma(q/2+1)^{2}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(d+q+1)}\frac{2^{(d+q)+2}}{\pi}

Thus, for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r_{\varphi_{q}}\coloneqq 1,

we have

\varphi_{q}(x)\leq C_{\varphi_{q}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{-(d+1)}

with

C_{\varphi_{q}}=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\mathrm{B}(d/2,q+1)}\pi{}^{d/2-2}\Gamma(q/2+1)^{2}2^{3(d+q)+4}.

Hence, substituting these estimates into (
28
) gives

\displaystyle(r+\delta)^{-1}r_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\left\{2^{2+d}3\{1+(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}\}\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\lambda_{m}d\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)\eta^{-1}C_{\varphi_{q}}\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2+1)}\right\}^{1/d}(1\lor\delta^{-1/d})

\displaystyle=\left\{2^{4d+3q+7}3\{1+(1+\theta)\tau^{-\theta}\}\left(\tau^{2}+1\right)^{q/2}\lambda_{m}\bigl(c_{m}+1\bigr)\eta^{-1}\frac{\pi^{d-2}\Gamma(q/2+1)^{2}}{\mathrm{B}(d/2,q+1)}\right\}^{1/d}(1\lor\delta^{-1/d}).

∎

Corollary B.2

(Approximation from
B.3
belongs to an RKHS)
.

Let
\theta\in[0,1]
.
Let
\ell(x,y)=w_{\theta-1}(x)w_{\theta-1}(y)\Phi(x-y)
be a kernel
defined by
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}
with continuous non-vanishing generalized Fourier transform
\hat{\Phi}
.
Consider the approximation
T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}
in Lemma
B.3

defined by the density
\varphi=\varphi_{q}
in Lemma
G.7
.
Then, each component of
w_{\theta-1}T_{\varphi,\rho}\tilde{v}
is an element
of RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}.

Proof.

The conclusion follows from Lemma
G.7

and the RKHS norm expression of
Wendland 2004

(see Lemma
G.8
for a concrete bound
on the norm).
∎

Appendix C
Topological equivalence between KSD- and Wasserstein
convergence

In Section
3.1
, we established the equivalence
between KSD- and
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
This appendix presents proofs for the results presented therein.

C.1
Proof of
3.1
:
Theorem 3.1

(Vanishing KSD implies
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

Here, we provide a proof for Theorem
3.1
.
Recall that
q
-Wasserstein convergence of a sequence is
equivalent to its weak convergence and
q
th moment uniform
integrability. We therefore aim to show that the suggested kernel
choices induce KSDs such that their convergence to zero implies the latter pair of conditions.

3.2
(with
3.1
) specifies conditions
for enforcing uniform integrability. Thus, the proof is complete
if we additionally establish conditions for the KSD to control weak
convergence.

Our proof relies on the following result of

[
]

:

Theorem C.1

(
Adaptation of
Barp et al. 2024
, Theorem 6
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}({\cal H}_{K})

is bounded
P
-separating, i.e., if
Q\in{\cal P},
and
\int h\differential Q=\int h\differential P

for all
h\in\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
with
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lvert h(x)\rvert<\infty
,
then we have
Q=P.
Then, for any tight sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
,
we have
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
iff
Q_{n}
converges
weakly to
P
as
n\to\infty.

Since the uniform integrability implies the tightness of a sequence,
it suffices to show that the kernel of the form (
9
)
can also induce the bounded
P
-separation property. We apply the
recipes provided in
Barp et al. 2024
.
We divide the proof into two parts according to the growth exponent
q_{m}
of diffusion matrix
\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

(see
2
).

Before proceeding with the proofs, we define some concepts and then provide a support lemma.
Recall that
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) denotes the set of continuously differentiable
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions that are compactly supported (resp. bounded) and have compactly supported partial derivatives (resp. bounded partial derivatives).
We equip
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the same norm as
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
Section

1
).
We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\beta}
the set
\mathcal{C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
equipped with the strict topology
[
Buck 1958
,
Barp et al. 2024
, Appendix A]
.
The topological dual of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We say
\mathcal{H}_{K}
is
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic if it uniquely embeds the elements of
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into itself
[see
Barp et al. 2024
, Appendix C for the definition of embeddability]
.

Lemma C.1

(Matrix-valued tilting preserves characteristicity)
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})

such that
A(x)
is invertible for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Suppose
a matrix-valued kernel
K
with
{\cal H}_{K}\subset{\cal C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic. Then,

K_{A}(x,y)=A(x)K(x,y)A(y)^{\top}
is also
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.

Proof.

Define
\phi_{A}:f\mapsto Af
, which is a continuous map from
\mathcal{C}_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})_{\beta}
to itself.
We also have
\phi_{A}(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, since for any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
g\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):x\mapsto A(x)^{-1}f(x)
satisfying
\phi_{A}(g)=f
.
The claim follows from
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
being dense in
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and applying
Barp et al. 2024
with
\phi=\phi_{A}
.
∎

C.1.1
Linear case
q_{m}=0

We prove the claim for the two suggested kernels.

Proof for kernel
L^{(1)}

We first observe that
2
, together with
the assumptions
\limsup_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\to\infty}\lVert m^{-1}(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty

and
\lVert\langle\nabla,m(x)\rangle\rVert_{2}=O(\lVert x\rVert_{2})
,
implies that we may take some constant
C>0
such that

\lVert\mathbf{s}_{p}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq Cw_{1}(x)

(29)

where
\mathbf{s}_{p}(x)=\nabla\log p(x)
and
w_{1}(x)=(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
.

We use
Barp et al. 2024
to show that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
is bounded
P
-separating.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{L_{0}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by
Barp et al. 2024
,
the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{L_{0}}
is continuously embedded into
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; therefore, the
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universality assumption is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristicity by
Simon-Gabriel and Schölkopf 2018
.
Then, we examine the following kernel

m(x)w_{q-1}(x)L^{(1)}(x,y)w_{q-1}(y)m(y)^{\top}=\frac{m(x)L_{0}(x,y)m(y)^{\top}}{w_{1}(x)^{2}w_{1}(y)^{2}}=\frac{L_{m/w_{1}}(x,y)}{w_{1}(x)w_{1}(y)}

where
L_{m/w_{1}}(x,y)\coloneqq m(x)L_{0}(x,y)m(y)^{\top}/\{w_{1}(x)w_{1}(y)\}
.
The scaled diffusion matrix
m/w_{1}
is bounded and has bounded derivatives; the kernel
L_{m/w_{1}}
has its RKHS included in
{\cal C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and it is again
{\cal D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic by Lemma
C.1
.
Using (
29
), we apply
Barp et al. 2024
with
\theta(x)=Cw_{1}(x)

and
K(x,y)=C^{2}L_{m/w_{1}}(x,y)
, which implies the

P
-bounded separability of
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{L}^{(1)}})

where
\tilde{L}^{(1)}(x,y)=w_{q-1}(x)L^{(1)}(x,y)w_{q-1}(y)
.
It follows from
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{L}^{(1)}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})

that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
is also
P
-bounded separating.
Hence, we have established that
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(\cdot)
controls tight
weak convergence.

It then suffices to show that
3.2

applies, as it then follows that the KSD also enforces
q
th
moment uniform integrability.
This claim immediately holds by the
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universality of kernel

\frac{L_{0}(x,y)}{{w}_{q+1}(x){w}_{q+1}(y)},

which can be verified by the same argument for
L_{m/w_{1}}
above.
Hence, we have proved the claim for
L=L^{(1)}
.
∎

Proof for kernel
L^{(2)}

Weak convergence control is shown as in the proof of
Barp et al. 2024
.
By
Barp et al. 2024
, there exists a translation-invariant

{\cal D}_{L}^{1}
-characteristic kernel
\ell_{s}\in{\cal C}^{(1,1)},

and a positive-definite function
f
, satisfying
1/f\in{\cal C}^{1}

and
\lvert f(x)\rvert\lor\lVert\nabla f(x)\rVert_{2}=O\bigl(\exp(-\sum_{\dimidx=1}^{\datdim}\sqrt{\verts{x_{\dimidx}}})\bigr)
,
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\ell_{f}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
with
\ell_{f}(x,y)=f(x)\ell_{s}(x,y)f(y).

Let
L_{f}=\ell_{f}\mathrm{Id}
and
L_{s}=\ell_{s}\mathrm{Id}
.
Then, we have

m(x)w_{q-1}(x)L_{f}(x,y)w_{q-1}(y)m(y)^{\top}=w_{1}(x)^{-1}\underbrace{\tilde{m}(x)L_{s}(x,y)\tilde{m}(y)^{\top}}_{\tilde{L}_{s}(x,y)}w_{1}(y)^{-1},

(30)

where
\tilde{m}(x)=f(x)w_{1}(x)w_{q-1}(x)m(x)
.
By the root-exponential decay of
f
and the growth conditions
on
m
(Assumption
2
and
\lVert\langle\nabla,m(x)\rangle\rVert_{2}=O(\lVert x\rVert_{2})
),
the kernel
\tilde{L}_{s}
is bounded and has bounded derivatives;
it also follows from
Barp et al. 2024

and Lemma
C.1
that
\tilde{L}_{s}

is
\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-characteristic.
Since
\lVert\mathbf{s}_{p}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq Cw_{1}(x)
,
applying
Barp et al. 2024
as in the proof for
L^{(1)}
,
we have that the Stein kernel induced by the LHS of (
30
)
is bounded, and the corresponding RKHS is bounded
P
-separating,
thus controlling tight weak convergence to
P.

It follows from
\mathcal{H}_{\ell_{f}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})
is also bounded
P
-separating.

The
q
-growth approximation property of
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})

follows from the
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universality of
L^{(2)}
and
3.2
.
The proof is complete.
∎

C.1.2
Quadratic case
q_{m}=1

The generalized Fourier transform
\hat{\Phi}
acts as the density (with respect to the Lebesgue measure) of the finite measure appearing in the integral representation of
\Phi
via Bochner’s theorem
[
Wendland 2004
, Theorem 6.6]
.
With
\hat{\Phi}
non-vanishing, the measure has full support, and therefore the kernel
\ell
is
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}
-universal
[
Simon-Gabriel and Schölkopf 2018
, Theorem 17]
.
Moreover, there exists a translation-invariant kernel with root-exponentially tilting
[
Barp et al. 2024
, Theorem 8]
.
The suggested kernel form
L^{(2)}
is the same as the linear growth case up to the tilting
1/\sqrt{\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}}
, which does not affect the root-exponential tilting.
Thus, the rest of the proof follows essentially the same.
∎

C.2
Wasserstein convergence implies KSD convergence

This appendix provides proofs for
3.2
and
3.2
and
3.3

C.2.1
Proof of
3.2
:
Proposition 3.2

(KSD detects
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

The proof essentially follows
Barp et al. 2024
.
Suppose
Q_{n}\to P
in
q
-Wasserstein convergence.
Then, for sufficiently large
n_{0}\geq 1
, we have
Q_{n}\in{\cal P}_{q}
for each
n\geq n_{0}
. Thus, without loss of generality, we may assume
Q_{n}\in{\cal P}_{q}
for all
n\geq 1
.
For
Q\in{\cal P}_{q}
, let
\tilde{Q}
be a measure defined as
\tilde{Q}(A)=\int_{A}(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q})\differential Q(x)
.
Then, the sequence
(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2},\dots)
weakly converges to
\tilde{P}
.
Since the product measure of finite nonnegative measures is weakly continuous
[
Berg et al. 1984
, Theorem 3.3, p.47]
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(Q_{n})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{k_{p,K}(x,y)}{(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q})(1+\lVert y\rVert_{2}^{q})}\tilde{Q}_{n}\otimes\tilde{Q}_{n}\bigl(\differential(x,y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\to\int\frac{k_{p,K}(x,y)}{(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q})(1+\lVert y\rVert_{2}^{q})}\tilde{P}\otimes\tilde{P}\bigl(\differential(x,y)\bigr)=\mathrm{KSD}_{K,P}(P)^{2}=0\ (n\to\infty),

where, in the limit, we have used the continuity and the boundedness of the integrand, derived respectively from the continuity of
k_{p,K}
and the
q
-growth of
x\mapsto\sqrt{k_{p,K}(x,x)}
.
∎

C.2.2
Proof of
3.2
:
Corollary 3.2

(User-friendly conditions for detecting
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

For a matrix-valued kernel
K
, let us define

K_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
and

K_{12}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d\times d}
by

K_{1}(a,b)=\nabla_{x}K(x,y)|_{x=a,y=b}\ \text{and}\ K_{12}(a,b)=\nabla_{x}\otimes\nabla_{y}K(x,y)|_{x=a,y=b}.

By the derivative reproducing property
[
Barp et al. 2024
, Lemma 4]
, these functions satisfy the following:

\displaystyle(K_{1}(a,b))_{i,j,l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{1,i}K_{jl}(a,b)

(31)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\langle e_{j},\partial_{i,1}\partial_{i,2}K(a,a)e_{j}\rangle}\sqrt{\langle e_{l},K(b,b)e_{l}\rangle}

and

\displaystyle(K_{12}(a,b))_{p,i,q,j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{1,p}\partial_{2,i}K_{qj}(a,b)

(32)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\langle e_{q},\partial_{1,p}\partial_{2,p}K(a,a)e_{q}\rangle}\sqrt{\langle e_{j},\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(b,b)e_{j}\rangle},

where
e_{i}
denotes the
i
th standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

For a matrix-valued function
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we define
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}A=\langle\nabla,pmA\rangle/p
.
The Stein kernel is then expressed as
k_{p,K_{m}}=\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m,2}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m,1}K
, where
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m,1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m,2}
denote applying
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}
to the first and second arguments, respectively.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}A(x)=2\langle b(x),A(x)\rangle+\langle m(x)^{\top}\nabla,A(x)\rangle
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} k_{p,K_{m}}(x,y)&=4\langle b(x),K(x,y)b(y)\rangle+2\langle b(y),\langle m(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1},K(x,y)\rangle\rangle\\
&\hphantom{=}+2\langle b(x),\langle m(y)^{\top}\nabla_{2},K(x,y)\rangle\rangle+\langle m(y)^{\top}\nabla_{2},\langle m(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1},K(x,y)\rangle\rangle.\end{aligned}

(33)

In particular, from
Equation

33
, we obtain

\displaystyle k_{p,K_{m}}(x,x)

\displaystyle\leq 4\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}^{2}\lVert K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+4d\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lVert K_{1}(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}+d\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\lVert K_{12}(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}^{2}\lVert K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+4d^{2}\lVert b(x)\rVert_{2}\sqrt{\lVert K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lVert\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}\quad+d^{2}\lVert m(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lVert\partial_{i,1}\partial_{1,2}K(x,x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

where the second inequality follows from (
31
) and (
32
). Thus,
under
2
and the supposed conditions on the kernel, we have
the following evaluation:

\displaystyle\sqrt{k_{p,K_{m}}(x,x)}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{0}\lambda_{b}(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})^{q-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{b}\lambda_{m}C_{0}C_{1}}d^{5/4}\sqrt{(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{m}+1})(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})^{2q-q_{m}-1}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq}+C_{1}d\lambda_{m}(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{m}+1})(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2})^{q-q_{m}-1},

which concludes the proof.
∎

C.2.3
Proof of
3.3
:
Corollary 3.3

(KSD-Wasserstein equivalence)
.

Note that translation invariant kernels
\ell
have both
\ell(x,x)
and
\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}\ell(x,x)
bounded.
Also, since
\mathcal{H}_{L_{0}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}
, the boundedness of
L_{0}(x,x)
and
\partial_{1,i}\partial_{2,i}L_{0}(x,x)
holds
[
Barp et al. 2024
, Lemma 3 (a)-(b)]
. The required growth conditions for the three kernel choices can be easily checked from the form (
5
).
∎

C.2.4
Proof of
3.4
:
Corollary 3.4

(User-friendly KSD-Wasserstein equivalence)
.

The suggested kernel form
k_{\textup{IMQ${}^{+}$}}(x,y)
corresponds to setting
\ell
in
L^{(2)}
to the IMQ kernel
k_{\textup{IMQ}}
.
The IMQ kernel is a translation-invariant kernel defined by an infinitely differentiable function
\Phi
, and according to
Wendland 2004
,
has a continuous nonvanishing generalized Fourier transform

\hat{\Phi}(\omega)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\lVert\omega\rVert_{2}^{-(d-1)/2}K_{(d-1)/2}(\lVert\omega\rVert_{2}),

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function, and
K_{(d-1)/2}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order
(d-1)/2
.
Thus,
k_{\mathrm{IMQ}}
satisfies the requirements in
3.1
.
∎

Appendix D
Coercive functions and
q
-Wassserstein convergence

As mentioned in
3
in the main text, we may obtain a KSD that enforces
q
th moment uniform integrability by assuming a higher-order moment.
This appendix provides results relevant to this claim.

We begin with two definitions:

Definition 7

(Coercive functions)
.

Let
q_{0}\in[0,\infty).
A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is said to be coercive of order
q_{0}
if there exists

r_{0}>0
such that
\inf_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}}f(x)>\nu
for
some
\nu\in\mathbb{R};
and
f(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q_{0}}

for some
\eta>0
if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{0}.

Definition 8

(Integrability rate)
.

For
Q\in{\cal P}_{q}
and
\varepsilon>0,
we define the

q
th moment integrability rate by

R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)\coloneqq\inf\left\{r\geq 1:\int_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q(x)\leq\varepsilon\right\}

An order-
q_{0}
coercive function approximates the
q_{0}
-growth
outside a ball, while it is only assumed to be bounded below inside
the ball. The integrability rate above represents the radius of
a ball, outside of which the tail moment integral becomes negligible.
Note that
Q\in{\cal P}_{q}
is equivalent to having
R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)<\infty

for each
\varepsilon>0.
In particular, if a sequence
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}

does not have uniformly integrable
q
-th moments, the integrability
rate
R_{q}(Q_{n},\varepsilon)
diverges. The case

q=0
corresponds to the tightness rate used by
Gorham and Mackey 2017
.

We first show that if the Stein RKHS admits a coercive function, the
KSD then enforces the uniform integrability.

Lemma D.1

(KSD upper-bounds the integrability rate)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty).
Let

\mathcal{H}_{K}
be the RKHS of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions defined
by a matrix-valued kernel
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

for which
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}({\cal H}_{K})
exists. Assume that the
Stein RKHS
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}({\cal H}_{K})
contains a coercive function
of order
q+\theta
for some
\theta>0.
Then, for sufficiently
small
\varepsilon>0,
we have

\displaystyle R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\left\{2\left(1+\frac{q}{\theta}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta\varepsilon}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{\theta}\lor\frac{q}{\theta}}.

Thus, for a sequence
(Q_{1},Q_{2}\dots)\subset{\cal P}

we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})<\infty\Rightarrow\limsup_{n\to\infty}R_{q}(Q_{n},\varepsilon)<\infty.

In particular, if the sequence
\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\dots\}
does not have
uniformly integrable
q
th moments, then
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})

diverges as
n\to\infty.

Proof.

Let
f(x)=\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}1\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\}.
We consider
the integral

\int_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q(x)=\int f(x)\differential Q(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}Q(\{f(x)>t\})\differential t.

By dividing the range of the integral, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}Q(\{f(x)>t\})\differential t

\displaystyle=r^{q}Q(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\})+\int_{r^{q}}^{\infty}Q(\{f(x)>t\})\differential t

\displaystyle=r^{q}Q(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\})+\int_{r}^{\infty}Q\bigl\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>t^{1/q}\bigr\}\differential t,

where we regard the second term as zero when
q=0.

We evaluate the tail probabilities in terms of the KSD. By assumption,
we have a function
v\in\mathcal{H}_{K}
such that
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v
is
a coercive function of order
q+\theta;
i.e., there exists

r_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q+\theta}

for
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{0}
and
\inf_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)>\nu,

where
\eta>0
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}.
Following the proof of
Gorham and Mackey 2017
,
we define
\gamma(r)=\inf\{{\cal T}_{P}^{m}v(x)-\nu:\lVert x\rVert_{2}\geq r\}.

Define
\text{$r_{\gamma}$}\coloneqq r_{0}\lor 2(\lvert\nu\rvert/\eta)^{1/(q+\theta)}.

It is straightforward to check that for
r\geq r_{\gamma},
we have

\gamma(r)\geq\eta r^{q+\theta}/2.
By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle Q(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle>r\})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}\gamma(\lVert X\rVert_{2})}{\gamma(r)}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q}[\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(X)-\nu]}{\gamma(r)}\leq\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\gamma(r)}.

These observations yield the following estimate of the above integral:

\displaystyle\int_{\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q(x)

\displaystyle=r^{q}Q(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r\})+1_{\{q>0\}}\int_{r}^{\infty}Q(\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>t^{1/q}\})\differential t

\displaystyle\leq r^{q}\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\gamma\bigl(r\bigr)}+1_{\{q>0\}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\gamma\bigl(t\bigr)}\differential t

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta r^{\theta}}+21_{\{q>0\}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta t^{1+\theta/q}}\differential t

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta}\left(\frac{1}{r^{\theta}}+\frac{q}{\theta}\frac{1}{r^{\theta/q}}1_{\{q>0\}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(1+\frac{q}{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta}\frac{1}{r^{\theta\land\theta/q}}

where we assume
r\geq r_{\gamma}\lor 1
and use the convention
\theta/0=\infty.

Therefore, for
\epsilon>0,
by taking sufficiently large
r_{\epsilon}\geq 1

such that

2\left(1+\frac{q}{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta}\frac{1}{r_{\varepsilon}^{\theta\land\theta/q}}\leq\varepsilon\ \text{and}\ r_{\varepsilon}\geq r_{\gamma},

we have

\int_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r_{\epsilon}}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}\differential Q(x)\leq{\cal\varepsilon}.

Therefore, the
q
th moment integrability rate
R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)

satisfies

R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)\leq\left\{2\left(1+\frac{q}{\theta}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q)-\nu}{\eta\varepsilon}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{\theta}\lor\frac{q}{\theta}}\vee r_{\gamma}.

For sufficiently small
\varepsilon,
the KSD term dominates
r_{\gamma}.

Thus, the claim has been proved.
∎

A similar bound on the tightness rate was obtained by
Gorham and Mackey 2017
.

D.1
establishes a result similar to

3.1
. A major difference
is that
3.1
does not require
P\in{\cal P}_{q+\theta}
.

D.1
essentially states that the KSD controls a higher-order moment;
that is, under the coercivity assumption, the condition
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})<\infty
implies
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim Q_{n}}[\lVert X\rVert_{2}^{q+\theta}]<\infty
, and hence controls
q
th moment uniform integrability.
Note that while we may take as small
\theta
as possible for enforcing uniform integrablity, assuming a higher order moment provides a benefit of a tighter bound on
R_{q}(Q,\varepsilon)
.

We can construct an RKHS admitting a coercive function.

Lemma D.2

(Existence of coercive functions)
.

Let
q\in[0,\infty).
Assume
the dissipativity condition in Assumption
1

with
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{0}.
Suppose Assumption
2

holds for some constants
c_{m}>0
and
q_{m}\in\{0,1\}
;
if
q_{m}=1,
additionally assume
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q-2).

Let
v(x)=-w_{q-2}(x)x
with
w_{q-2}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q-2)/2}
.
Then, there exists
r>0

\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q},

and
\min_{\lVert x\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)>-\infty,

where
\eta
is determined by the following quantities:
\alpha,

\beta_{1},

\beta_{0},

\lambda_{m},

\tau,

q_{m},

and
q.

Proof.

The proof proceeds as in that of Lemma
B.1
;
specifically, the argument for the regime
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>r+\delta

in Lemma
B.1
shows that there exists

\tilde{r}>0
such that
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)\geq\eta\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q}

for some
\eta>0
if
\lVert x\rVert_{2}>\tilde{r}.
Since
v
is
continuously differentiable,
\mathcal{T}_{P}^{m}v(x)
is continuous,
which implies that there exists a minimum inside the ball of radius

\tilde{r}.

∎

Lemma D.3

(Stein RKHS admits coercive functions)
.

Fix
q\in[0,\infty)
.
Define
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by

K(x,y)=L(x,y)+w_{q-2}(x)k_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)w_{q-2}(y)\mathrm{Id},

(34)

where
w_{q-2}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q-2)/2}

with
\tau>0
;

L
is a matrix-valued kernel with
\mathcal{H}_{L}\subset\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and

k_{\mathrm{lin}}(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle+\tau^{2}
.
Assume the dissipativity condition in Assumption
1

with
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{0}.
Assume the growth conditions in
Assumption
2
. If
q_{m}
in Assumption

2
is equal to
1,
additionally assume that

\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q-2).
Then,
{\cal T}_{P}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{K})

admits a coercive function of order
q
.

Proof.

The function
v=-\tilde{w}(x)x
is an element of the RKHS of the
second kernel in (
34
). The conclusion follows
from Lemma
D.2
.
∎

We end this appendix by establishing a result analogous to Theorem

3.1
.

Theorem D.1

(KSD convergence implies
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

Fix
q,\theta\in(0,\infty)
.
Suppose
P\in{\cal P}_{q+\theta}
.
Define base kernel K as in (
9
)
using tilting
w_{q+\theta-1}(x)=\bigl(\tau^{2}+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}\bigr)^{(q+\theta-1)/2}
.
Specify the kernel
L
by either of the following two kernels:

\displaystyle L^{(1)}(x,y)=\frac{L_{0}(x,y)}{{w}_{q+\theta+q_{m}}(x)w_{q+\theta+q_{m}}(y)},\ L^{(2)}(x,y)=\frac{\ell(x,y)}{w_{q_{m}}(x)w_{q_{m}}(y)}\mathrm{Id},

where

L_{0}
is
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-universal, and

\ell
is a scalar-valued translation-invariant
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{1}
-universal
kernel.
If
q_{m}=1
in
2
, assume
\alpha>\lambda_{m}(q+\theta-2)
.
Then, for any sequence
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{P}
, under
2
and
1
,

Q_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
if
\mathrm{KSD}_{K_{m},P}(Q_{n})\to 0
(
n\to\infty)
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Theorem
3.1
:
the kernel choice ensures weak convergence control, along with the
uniform integrability guarantee by Lemma
D.3
.
∎

Appendix E
Polynomial functions are pseudo-Lipschitz functions

We show that the pseudo-Lipschitz class
{\cal F}_{q}
, used in
Section

3.2
, suffices for characterizing
convergence in moments.

Lemma E.1

(Monomial functions are pseudo-Lipschitz)
.

Let
q\geq 1
be an integer. Let
\bm{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{d})\in\{0,\dots,q\}^{d}

be a multi-index such that
\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}=q\geq 1.

Then,
x^{\bm{q}}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{q_{i}}

is pseudo-Lipschitz of order
q-1.
Its pseudo-Lipschitz
constant
\left\|x^{\bm{q}}\right\|_{\mathrm{PL},q-1}
is bounded
by
(1\lor q/2)\lVert\bm{q}\rVert_{2}
.
Moreover, it satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\lVert\nabla^{j}x^{\bm{q}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}}

\displaystyle\leq d^{j/2}\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\frac{q_{i}!}{\{1_{\{q_{i}\geq j\}}(q_{i}-j)\}!}.

Thus,
x\mapsto c_{q,d}^{-1}x^{\bm{q}}
is an element of
\mathcal{F}_{q}
(see
Section

3.2.1
)
where

c_{q,d}=(1\lor q/2)\lVert\bm{q}\rVert_{2}\lor\max_{j=2,3}d^{j/2}1\{q\geq j\}\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\frac{q_{i}!}{\{1_{\{q_{i}\geq j\}}(q_{i}-j)\}!},

and
0!\coloneqq 1
.

Proof.

Let
f_{\bm{q}}:x\mapsto x^{\bm{q}}
. Note that we have

\lVert\nabla f_{\bm{q}}(x)\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\bm{q}\rVert_{2}\lVert x\rVert^{q-1}_{2}
.
Thus, by the mean value theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert x^{\bm{q}}-y^{\bm{q}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{q}\rVert_{2}\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\lVert tx+(1-t)y\rVert^{q-1}_{2}\differential t

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bm{q}\rVert_{2}(1\lor q/2)\bigl(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}+\lVert y\rVert_{2}^{q-1}\bigr)\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}.

Next we check the growth of the

j
-th derivatives. We assume
q\geq j
below, as the derivatives
are zero otherwise. Note that we have

\displaystyle(\nabla^{j}x^{\bm{n}})_{l_{1},\dots,l_{j}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{q_{i}!}{(q_{i}-m_{i})!}\cdot x_{i}^{q_{i}-m_{i}}\cdot 1{\{q_{i}\geq m_{i}\}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\frac{q_{i}!}{\{1_{\{q_{i}\geq j\}}(q_{i}-j)\}!}

where
m_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{j}1\{q_{k}=i\}
, for at least one index
i
satisfies
q_{i}\geq m_{i}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\lVert\nabla^{j}x^{\bm{q}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}}

\displaystyle\leq d^{j/2}\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\frac{q_{i}!}{\{1_{\{q_{i}\geq j\}}(q_{i}-j)\}!}.

∎

Appendix F
Characterization of pseudo-Lipschitz metrics

In this appendix, we first prove that pseudo-Lipschitz IPMs on a general metric space metrize
q
-Wasserstein convergence
(see
Appendix

A
for the definition of
q
-Wasserstein convergence).
We then provide a proof of
3.3
.

We first define necessary concepts.

Definition 9

(Uniform integrability)
.

Let
({\cal X},d)
be a separable metric space.
A sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P}({\cal X})

is said to have uniformly integrable
q
th moments if

\lim_{r\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{d(x,x_{0})\geq r}d(x,x_{0})^{q}\differential P_{n}(x)=0

for some (and then any)
x_{0}\in{\cal X}.

Definition 10

(Pseudo-Lipschitz functions on general metric spaces)
.

Let
({\cal X},d)
be a metric space.
For
V:{\cal X}\to[0,\infty),
a real-valued function
f
on
{\cal X}

is said to be
V
-pseudo-Lipschitz if it satisfies

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{L},V}\coloneqq\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\left(1+V(x)+V(y)\right)d(x,y)}<\infty.

For
q\geq 0,
we say that a
V
-pseudo-Lipschitz function

f
is of
order

q
if
V
satisfies the
following conditions for some point
x_{0}
in
{\cal X}
: (a) for
any
x\in{\cal X},
and some
A_{0}\geq 0,

A_{1}>0,
we have
V(x)\leq A_{0}+A_{1}d(x,x_{0})^{q},

and (b) if
q>0,
there exists
B>0
and
R_{V}\geq 0

such that
V(x)>Bd(x,x_{0})^{q}
if
d(x,x_{0})>R_{V}.

The following lemma provides a trivial example of a pseudo-Lipschitz function.

Lemma F.1

(Metric power is pseudo-Lipschitz)
.

Let
d
be a metric on a set
{\cal X}.

For
q\in[1,\infty)
and
x_{0}\in{\cal X},
the function
x\mapsto d(x,x_{0})^{q}

is
V
-pseudo-Lipschitz with
V(x)=d(x,x_{0})^{q-1};
thus,
it is of order
q-1.
We have
\lVert d(\cdot,x_{0})\rVert_{\mathrm{L},V}=1

if
1\leq q<2,
and
\lVert d(\cdot,x_{0})\rVert_{\mathrm{L},V}\leq q/2

if
q\geq 2.

Proof.

The case
q=1
follows from the triangle inequality. Consider

q>1.
By the mean value theorem, for any two real numbers

a,b
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert a^{q}-b^{q}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq q\lvert a-b\rvert\int_{0}^{1}\lvert(1-t)a+tb\rvert^{q-1}\differential t

If
1<q<2,
by the triangle inequality and
(\alpha+\beta)^{q-1}\leq\alpha^{q-1}+\beta^{q-1}

for
\alpha,\beta\geq 0,
we have

\displaystyle\lvert a^{q}-b^{q}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\lvert a\rvert^{q-1}+\lvert b\rvert^{q-1}\right)\lvert a-b\rvert,

whereas if
q>2,
by Jensen’s inequality,

\lvert a^{q}-b^{q}\rvert\leq\frac{q}{2}\left(1+\lvert a\rvert^{q-1}+\lvert b\rvert^{q-1}\right)\lvert a-b\rvert.

Thus, since
\lvert d(x,x_{0})-d(y,x_{0})\rvert\leq d(x,y),
letting
a=d(x,x_{0})

and
b=d(y,x_{0})
gives

\lvert d(x,x_{0})^{q}-d(y,y_{0})^{q}\rvert\leq C\bigl(1+d(x,x_{0})^{q-1}+d(y,y_{0})^{q-1}\bigr)d(x,y),

where
C=1
if
1\leq q<2,
and
C=q/2
if
q\geq 2.

∎

The following proposition shows that
q
-Wasserstein convergence is induced by the IPM that is defined
by a pseudo-Lipschitz class.

Proposition F.1

(Pseudo-Lipschitz metric metrizes
q
-Wasserstein convergence)
.

Let
({\cal X},d)
be a separable metric space.
Fix
q\geq 1
.
Let
P\in{\cal P}_{q}(\mathcal{X})
,
and
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}

be the IPM defined by the subset
\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)=\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}:\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{L},V}\leq 1\}

of
V
-pseudo Lipschitz functions of order
q-1.
For a
sequence of probability measures
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P}({\cal X}),

the following statements are equivalent: (a)
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}(P,P_{n})\to 0

as
n\to\infty,
and (b)
P_{n}
converges to
P
in
the sense of
q
-Wasserstein convergence.

Proof.

We may assume
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal P}_{q}(\mathcal{X})
, since if either of (a) or (b) holds, then
(P_{n})_{n\geq n_{0}}\subset{\cal P}_{q}(\mathcal{X})
for some
n_{0}\geq 1
.

\text{(a)}\ \Rightarrow\ \text{(b):}
By
Villani 2003
,
it suffices to show that
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges
weakly to
P
and
\mathbb{E}_{P_{n}}[d(\cdot,x_{0})^{q}]\to\mathbb{E}_{P}[d(\cdot,x_{0})^{q}].

For the first claim, note that the set
\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)

contains the set
\mathrm{BL}=\{f:{\cal X}\to\mathbb{R}:\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\coloneqq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{L}}\leq 1\}

where
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in{\cal X}}\lvert f(x)\rvert
and
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathrm{L}}=\sup_{x\neq y}\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert/d(x,y).

The convergence
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}}(P,P_{n})\to 0

thus implies
d_{\mathrm{BL}}(P,P_{n})\to 0.
The sequence
converges to
P
weakly, since
d_{\mathrm{BL}}
metrizes
weak convergence
[
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 11.3.3]
. To prove the second
claim, note that by Definition
10
, for
q>1,

there exist a point
x_{0}\in{\cal X},
and constants
B>0,

R_{V}\geq 0

such that
V(x)\geq Bd(x,x_{0})^{q-1}
if
d(x,x_{0})>R_{V}.

Then,
f_{q}(x)=d(x,x_{0})^{q}
satisfies, for
any
x,y\in{\cal X},

\displaystyle\frac{\lvert f_{q}(x)-f_{q}(y)\rvert}{\bigl(1+V(x)+V(y)\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+f_{q-1}(x)+f_{q-1}(x)}{1+V(x)+V(y)}\frac{\lvert f_{q}(x)-f_{q}(y)\rvert}{1+f_{q-1}(x)+f_{q-1}(y)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2\sup_{x\in{\cal X}}\frac{d(x,x_{0})^{q-1}}{1+V(x)}\right)\frac{\lvert f_{q}(x)-f_{q}(y)\rvert}{1+f_{q-1}(x)+f_{q-1}(y)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}\cdot\frac{\lvert f_{q}(x)-f_{q}(y)\rvert}{1+f_{q-1}(x)+f_{q-1}(y)}

with

C_{q}=\begin{cases}3&\text{if}\ q=1\\
\left(1+2\bigl(R_{V}^{q-1}\lor B^{-1})\right)&\text{if}\ q>1.\end{cases}

This observation together with
F.1
shows
that
f_{q}
is
V
-pseudo-Lipschitz. The conclusion follows
from
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}(P,P_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty.

\text{(b)}\ \Rightarrow\ \text{(a):}
Our goal is to show that the
following quantity can be made arbitrarily small by taking sufficiently
large
n:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\left|\int f\differential P_{n}-\int f\differential P\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\left|\int\bigl(f-f(x_{0})\bigr)\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)+\underbrace{\int f(x_{0})\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)}_{=0}\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q}(V)}\left|\int\bar{f}\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)\right|,

where
\bar{f}=f-f(x_{0}),
and
x_{0}
is a point such that
V(x)\leq A_{0}+A_{1}d(x,x_{0})^{q-1}

for any
x\in{\cal X},
and some
A_{0}\geq 0,

A_{1}>0.

As a preparatory step, we clarify some properties of
\bar{f}.
For
any
x,y\in{\cal X},
we have

\displaystyle\lvert\bar{f}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1+V(x_{0})+V(x)\bigr)d(x,x_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1+A_{0}+V(x_{0})+A_{1}d(x,x_{0})^{q-1}\bigr)d(x,x_{0})

and

\displaystyle\lvert\bar{f}(x)-\bar{f}(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1+V(x)+V(y)\bigr)d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1+2A_{0}+A_{1}d(x,x_{0})^{q-1}+A_{1}d(y,x_{0})^{q-1}\bigr)d(x,y).

These estimates imply the following: (i) the growth of
\bar{f}

is of
d(\cdot,x_{0})^{q},
and (ii) the restriction of
\bar{f}

to a closed ball
{\cal B}_{r}(x_{0})=\{x\in{\cal X}:d(x,x_{0})\leq r\}

is a bounded Lipschitz function. By
[
Dudley 2002
, Proposition 11.2.3]
,
the restricted function
\bar{f}_{|{\cal B}_{r}(x_{0})}
can
be extended to a function
\tilde{f}
on
{\cal X}
without changing
its bounded-Lipschitz norm. In particular, the bounded-Lipschitz norm

\lVert\tilde{f}\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
can be bounded by a constant depending
only on
r.

For
R>0,
consider
g_{R}(x)\coloneqq\bigl(1-d(x,{\cal B}_{R}(x_{0}))\bigr)_{+},

where
d(x,F)\coloneqq\inf_{x\in F}d(x,F)
for a set
F;
the function
satisfies
1_{{\cal B}_{R}(x_{0})}\leq g_{R}\leq 1_{{\cal B}_{R+1}(x_{0})},

and is Lipschitz by
[
Dudley 2002
, Proposition 11.2.2]
. For
f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V),

consider the bounded-Lipschitz extension
\tilde{f}
of
f-f(x_{0})

restricted to the closed ball
{\cal B}_{R+1}(x_{0}).
Since the
product of bounded Lipschitz functions is again bounded-Lipschitz

[
Dudley 2002
, Proposition 11.2.1]
, the function
\tilde{f}g_{R}

is bounded-Lipschitz with

\lVert\tilde{f}g_{R}\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq\lVert\tilde{f}\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\lVert g_{R}\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq C_{R},

where
C_{R}>0
is a constant that does not depend on the choice of
f
.

We are ready to bound the quantify of interest. For a given
R>0

and any
\varepsilon>0,
using the weak convergence assumption, take

n
large enough so that

d_{\mathrm{BL}}(P_{n},P)=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{BL}}\left|\int f\differential P_{n}-\int f\differential P\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{C_{R}},

which is possible as the bounded Lipschitz metric
d_{\mathrm{BL}}

metrizes weak convergence
[
Dudley 2002
, Theorem 11.3.3]
. The
definition of
R
has been left unspecified; by
Villani 2003
,
the sequence
\{P_{1},P_{2},\cdots,\}
has uniformly integrable
q
-th
moments, and we may therefore take
R\geq 1
such that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{\{d(x,x_{0})>R\}}d(x,x_{0})^{q}\differential P_{n}(x)\lor\int_{\{d(x,x_{0})>R\}}d(x,x_{0})^{q}\differential P(x)<\varepsilon.

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0,
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\left|\int\bar{f}\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\left|\int\bar{f}\{(1-g_{R})+g_{R}\}\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\int_{{\cal X}\setminus{\cal B}_{R}(x_{0})}\lvert\bar{f}\rvert\differential(P_{\seqidx}+P)+\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\left|\int\tilde{f}g_{R}\differential(P_{\seqidx}-P)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}\int_{{\cal X}\setminus{\cal B}_{R}(x_{0})}d(x,x_{0})^{q}\differential(P_{\seqidx}+P)(x)+C_{R}d_{\mathrm{BL}}(P_{n},P)

\displaystyle\leq(2C+1)\varepsilon,

where
C=1+A_{0}+A_{1}+V(x_{0}),
and the first inequality is due
to
\tilde{f}\equiv\bar{f}
on
\mathcal{B}_{R+1}(x_{0}).

∎

Remark
.

When
d
is the discrete metric
d(x,y)=1\{x\neq y\}
, the IPM defined by 1-
V
-pseudo-Lipschitz functions is (up to a multiplicative constant) the weighted total variation distance with weight
1+2V

[
Hairer and Mattingly 2011
, Lemma 2.1]
.
The above use of order-
(q-1)
-
V
-pseudo Lipschitz functions may be considered as an alternative weighted-seminorm generalization of the 1-Wasserstein distance.

F.1
Proof of
3.3
:
Proposition 3.3

(Pseudo-Lipschitz metric)
.

We address the following result for the class
{\cal F}_{q}
presented in the main text.
We first provide a proof for this result and then prove a lemma used in the proof.

See
3.3

Proof.

Suppose
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.
Then, by
F.1
with
V(x)=\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}
,
we have
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}(P,P_{n})\to 0
, leading to
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,P_{n})\to 0
as

d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,P_{n})\leq d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}(P,P_{n})
.
For the other direction, suppose
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,P_{n})\to 0
.
Then, by
F.2
with
\mu=P
,

d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(V)}(P,P_{n})\to 0
.
By another application of
F.1
, we have
P_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{q}}}{{\to}}P
.
∎

Lemma F.2

(IPM
d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}
upper-bounds
d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}}
; an extension of
Mackey and Gorham 2016
, Lemma 2.2
)
.

Fix
q\in[1,\infty)
.
Assume
P\in\mathcal{P}_{q}
.
Let
\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}=\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}^{q-1})
be a set of
1
-pseudo-Lipscitz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, the following relation holds:
if
q=1
,

\displaystyle W_{1}(P,Q)=d_{\mathrm{PL}_{0}}(P,Q)\leq 3\left\{d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}(P,Q)\lor\left(d_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}(P,Q)\sqrt{2}\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}]^{2}\right)^{1/3}\right\},

and if
q>1
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}}(P,Q)\leq C_{\mu}\bigl(1\lor d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)^{1\lor(q+1)/3}\bigr)d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)^{1/3}.

Here,
G
is a standard normal vector with
\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}^{k}]=2^{k/2}\Gamma\bigl((d+k)/2\bigr)/\Gamma(d/2)
for any
k\geq 1
;
and
C_{\mu}
is a constant that depends on
\mu\in\{P,Q\}\cap{\cal P}_{q-1}
, defined by

\displaystyle C_{\mu}=\left(\frac{A}{M_{\mu}}\right)^{1/3}\left[2M_{\mu}\left\{1\lor\left(\frac{A}{M_{\mu}}\right)^{(q+1)/3}\right\}+B(1+c_{q-1})\mathbb{E}_{G}\bigl[\lVert G\rVert_{2}\bigr]\right],

with

A=c_{q-1}\sqrt{2}(1+\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}^{2(q-1)}]^{1/2})
,

c_{q-1}=1\lor 2^{(q-1)-1}
,

B=\frac{(q-1)(q+1)}{2}\lor\frac{\lvert(q-1)(q-3)(q+7)\rvert}{4},

and

\displaystyle M_{\mu}=2\left[\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}]\left\{1+(1+c_{q-1})\int(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2})^{(q-1)/2}\differential\mu(x)\right\}+c_{q-1}\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}^{q}]\right].

Proof.

We address the case
q>1
since
q=1
is the statement of
Mackey and Gorham 2016
.
We may assume
Q\in{\cal P}_{q}
as otherwise the conclusion is trivial.
Fix
f\in\mathrm{PL}_{q-1}
.
We have

\displaystyle\left|\int f\differential(P -Q)\right|\leq\left|\int(f-f_{t})\differential(P -Q)\right|+\left\lvert\int f_{t}\differential(P-Q)\right\rvert,

where, for
t>0
,
f_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is a smoothed version of
f
, defined by
f_{t}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{G}[f(x+tG)]
using a standard normal vector
G
.
Below, we evaluate each term in the upper bound.

For the first term, by the pseudo-Lipschitzness of
f
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert f_{t}(x)-f(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{G}\bigl[(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}+\lVert x+tG\rVert_{2}^{q-1})\lVert tG\rVert_{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq t(C_{1}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}+\mathbb{E}_{G}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}])+t^{q}C_{2}

where
C_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{G}\bigl[\lVert G\rVert_{2}\bigr](1+c_{q-1})
,

C_{2}=c_{q-1}\mathbb{E}_{G}\bigl[\lVert G\rVert_{2}^{q}\bigr]
,
and
c_{q-1}=1\lor 2^{(q-1)-1}
.
Also, with
w(x)=(1+\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2})^{(q-1)/2}
,

\displaystyle\int\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}\differential Q(x)\leq\int w(x)\differential Q(x)\leq Bd_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)+\int w(x)\differential P(x)

where
B
is a constant such that
B^{-1}w\in\mathcal{F}_{q}
;
we may choose
B=\max((q-1)(q+1)/2,\lvert(q-1)(q-3)(q+7)\rvert/4)
by
K.2
.
Note that the same argument applies with the roles
Q
and
P
swapped.
Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int\bigl(f_{t}(x)-f(x)\bigr)\differential(P-Q)(x)\right\rvert\leq\int\lvert f_{t}(x)-f(x)\rvert\differential(P+Q)(x)

\displaystyle\leq tC_{1}\int\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}\differential(P+Q)(x)+2t\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}]+2t^{q}C_{2}

\displaystyle\leq tC_{1}\int\{\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{q-1}+w(x)\}\differential P(x)+tBC_{1}d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q)+2t\mathbb{E}[\lVert G\rVert_{2}]+2t^{q}C_{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{P}(t\lor t^{q})+tBC_{1}d_{\mathcal{F}_{q}}(P,Q),

where
